# Grumman F-14 Tomcat

Grumman F-14 Tomcat

Carrier-based multi-role fighter aircraft family

"F14" redirects here. For other uses, see [F14 (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F14_(disambiguation) "F14 (disambiguation)").

**F-14 Tomcat**

The **Grumman F-14 Tomcat** is an American [carrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_carrier "Aircraft carrier")-capable [supersonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supersonic_aircraft "Supersonic aircraft"), [twin-engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twinjet "Twinjet"), [tandem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tandem#Aviation "Tandem") two-seat, twin-tail, all-weather-capable [variable-sweep wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variable-sweep_wing "Variable-sweep wing") [fighter aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fighter_aircraft "Fighter aircraft"). The Tomcat was developed for the [United States Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy "United States Navy")'s Naval Fighter Experimental (VFX) program after the collapse of the [General Dynamics-Grumman F-111B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics%E2%80%93Grumman_F-111B "General Dynamics–Grumman F-111B") project. A large and well-equipped fighter, the F-14 was the first of the American [Teen Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teen_Series "Teen Series") fighters that were designed incorporating [air combat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerial_warfare "Aerial warfare") experience against smaller, more maneuverable [MiG fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Mikoyan_and_MiG_aircraft "List of Mikoyan and MiG aircraft") during the [Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War "Vietnam War").

The F-14 first flew on 21 December 1970 and made its first deployment in 1974 with the U.S. Navy aboard the aircraft carrier [USS _Enterprise_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Enterprise_(CVN-65) "USS Enterprise (CVN-65)"), replacing the [McDonnell Douglas F-4 Phantom II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F-4_Phantom_II "McDonnell Douglas F-4 Phantom II"). The F-14 served as the U.S. Navy's primary maritime [air superiority fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_superiority_fighter "Air superiority fighter"), fleet defense [interceptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interceptor_aircraft "Interceptor aircraft"), and tactical [aerial reconnaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerial_reconnaissance "Aerial reconnaissance") platform into the 2000s. The Low Altitude Navigation and Targeting Infrared for Night ( [LANTIRN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LANTIRN "LANTIRN")) pod system was added in the 1990s and the Tomcat began performing precision ground-attack missions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-USN_FF-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-USN_FF) The Tomcat was retired by the U.S. Navy on 22 September 2006, supplanted by the [Boeing F/A-18E/F Super Hornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_F/A-18E/F_Super_Hornet "Boeing F/A-18E/F Super Hornet"). Several retired F-14s have been put on display across the United States.

Having been exported to [Pahlavi Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran "Pahlavi Iran") under the [Western-aligned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc "Western Bloc") Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Reza_Pahlavi "Mohammad Reza Pahlavi") in 1976, F-14s were used as land-based interceptors by the [Imperial Iranian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_force_history_of_Iran#Imperial_era "Air force history of Iran"). Following the [Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Revolution "Iranian Revolution") in 1979, the [Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_of_Iran_Air_Force "Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force") used them during the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War "Iran–Iraq War"). Iran claimed their F-14s shot down at least 160 Iraqi aircraft during the war (with 55 of these confirmed),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Persian_Cats-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-Persian_Cats) while 16 Tomcats were lost, including seven losses to accidents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Persian_Cats-1)[\[3\]](#refnote-Persian_Cats)[&#8202;](#ref-back-4-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-4)

## Development

### Background

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/F-111B_NAN7-65.jpg/500px-F-111B_NAN7-65.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-111B_NAN7-65.jpg) The [F-111B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-111B "F-111B") was designed to fulfill the carrier-based interceptor role, but had weight and performance problems, and was not suited to the types of aerial combat that were predominant over Vietnam.

Beginning in the late 1950s, the U.S. Navy sought a long-range, high-endurance interceptor to defend its [carrier battle groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrier_battle_group "Carrier battle group") against long-range [anti-ship missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-ship_missile "Anti-ship missile") launched from the jet bombers and submarines of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union"). They outlined the idea of a Fleet Air Defense (FAD) aircraft with a more powerful radar and longer range missiles than the [F-4 Phantom II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-4_Phantom_II "F-4 Phantom II") to intercept both enemy bombers and missiles at very long range.[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-5) Studies into this concept led to the [Douglas F6D Missileer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_F6D_Missileer "Douglas F6D Missileer") project of 1959, but this large subsonic aircraft would have limited ability to evade supersonic fighters or defend itself once it fired its missiles, and the project was canceled in December 1961.[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-6)

The Navy still sought long-range defensive aircraft, but with higher performance than the Missileer. The Navy was directed to participate in the [Tactical Fighter Experimental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TFX_Program "TFX Program") (TFX) program with the [U.S. Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Air_Force "U.S. Air Force") (USAF) by [Secretary of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_Defense "United States Secretary of Defense") [Robert McNamara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_McNamara "Robert McNamara"), who favored versatile aircraft that could be shared by both services, reducing procurement and development costs. To this end, he had already directed the USAF to buy the F-4 Phantom II—which was developed for the Navy and could serve both as a [fighter-bomber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fighter-bomber "Fighter-bomber") and an [interceptor aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interceptor_aircraft "Interceptor aircraft")—instead of buying more [F-105 Thunderchief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-105_Thunderchief "F-105 Thunderchief") and [F-106 Delta Dart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-106_Delta_Dart "F-106 Delta Dart") aircraft to fill each respective role.[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-7)

The TFX had adequate speed, range and payload for the FAD role, but was designed primarily as a fighter-bomber and [interdictor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interdictor "Interdictor") that lacked the maneuverability and overall performance that the Navy expected. The Navy strenuously opposed the TFX as it feared compromises necessary for the Air Force's need for a low-level attack aircraft would adversely impact the aircraft's performance as a fighter. Their concerns were overridden, and the project went ahead as the [F-111B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-111B "F-111B"). Lacking recent experience in naval fighters, the F-111's main contractor, [General Dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics "General Dynamics"), partnered with [Grumman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman "Grumman") to provide the experience needed to develop a naval version. Weight and performance issues plagued the program, and with the F-111B in distress, Grumman began studying improvements and alternatives. In 1966, the Navy awarded Grumman a contract to begin studying advanced fighter designs. Grumman narrowed down these designs to its Model 303 design.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spick_p71-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-Spick_p71)

Through this same period, experience in Vietnam against the more agile [MiG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG "MiG") fighters demonstrated that the Phantom lacked the maneuverability needed to win in any engagement. This led to the [VFAX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFAX "VFAX") program to study new fighter aircraft that would either replace or supplant the Phantom in the fighter and ground-attack roles while the TFX worked the long-range interception role.[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-9) Grumman continued work on its 303 design and offered it to the Navy in 1967, which led to fighter studies by the Navy. The company continued to refine the design into 1968.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spick_p71-1)[\[8\]](#refnote-Spick_p71)

Around this time, [Vice Admiral Thomas F. Connolly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_F._Connolly "Thomas F. Connolly"), Deputy Chief of Naval Operations for Air Warfare, flew the developmental [F-111A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-111A "F-111A") variant on a flight and discovered that it had difficulty going supersonic and had poor carrier landing characteristics. He later testified before Congress about his concerns against the official Navy position and, in May 1968, Congress stopped funding for the F-111B, allowing the Navy to pursue an answer tailored to its requirements.[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-10)

### VFX

Free to choose their solution to the FAD requirement, VFAX ended in favor of a new design that would combine the two roles. In July 1968, the [Naval Air Systems Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Systems_Command "Naval Air Systems Command") (NAVAIR) issued a [request for proposals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Request_for_proposal "Request for proposal") (RFP) for the Naval Fighter Experimental (VFX) program. VFX called for a tandem two-seat, twin-engine air-to-air fighter with a maximum speed of Mach 2.2.[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-11) It would also have a built-in 20mm [M61 Vulcan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M61_Vulcan "M61 Vulcan") cannon and a secondary close air support role.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Woolridge-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-Woolridge) The VFX's air-to-air missiles would be either six [AIM-54 Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-54_Phoenix "AIM-54 Phoenix") or a combination of six [AIM-7 Sparrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-7_Sparrow "AIM-7 Sparrow") and four [AIM-9 Sidewinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-9_Sidewinder "AIM-9 Sidewinder") missiles. Bids were received from General Dynamics, Grumman, [Ling-Temco-Vought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ling-Temco-Vought "Ling-Temco-Vought"), [McDonnell Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas "McDonnell Douglas"), and [North American Rockwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockwell_International "Rockwell International");[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spick_p9-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-Spick_p9) four bids incorporated [variable-geometry wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variable-sweep_wing "Variable-sweep wing").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Woolridge-1)[\[12\]](#refnote-Woolridge)[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[N 1\]](#refnote-14)

The name "Tomcat" was partially chosen to pay tribute to Connolly, as the nickname "Tom's Cat" had already been widely used within the program during development to reflect Connolly's involvement, and now the moniker was adapted into an official name in line with the Grumman tradition of giving its fighter aircraft feline names. Changing it to _Tomcat_ associated the aircraft with the previous Grumman aircraft [Wildcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_F4F_Wildcat "Grumman F4F Wildcat"), [Hellcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_F6F_Hellcat "Grumman F6F Hellcat"), [Tigercat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_F7F_Tigercat "Grumman F7F Tigercat"), and [Bearcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_F8F_Bearcat "Grumman F8F Bearcat") propeller fighters along with the [Panther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_F9F_Panther "Grumman F9F Panther"), [Cougar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_F-9_Cougar "Grumman F-9 Cougar"), and [Tiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_F-11_Tiger "Grumman F-11 Tiger") jet fighters. Other names considered were _[Alley Cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feral_cat "Feral cat")_ (considered inappropriate due to sexual connotations) and _Seacat_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-15)[&#8202;](#ref-back-16-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-16)

### Full-scale development

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/F-14_Tomcat_prototypes_in_flight_c1972.jpg/500px-F-14_Tomcat_prototypes_in_flight_c1972.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14_Tomcat_prototypes_in_flight_c1972.jpg) Grumman's VFX entry was designed around the TF30 engine, AWG-9 radar and AIM-54 missile intended for the F-111B; this eventually became the F-14A.

McDonnell Douglas and Grumman were selected as finalists in December 1968. Grumman's 303E design was selected for the contract award in January 1969.[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-17) The design reused the [TF30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney_TF30 "Pratt & Whitney TF30") engines from the F-111B, though the Navy planned on replacing them with the [Pratt & Whitney F401-PW-400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney_F401 "Pratt & Whitney F401") engines under development for the Navy, along with the related [Pratt & Whitney F100](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney_F100 "Pratt & Whitney F100") for the USAF.[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-18) Though lighter than the F-111B, it was still the largest and heaviest U.S. fighter to fly from an aircraft carrier, a consequence of the requirement to carry the large [AWG-9 radar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AN/AWG-9 "AN/AWG-9") and AIM-54 Phoenix missiles (from the F-111B) and an internal fuel load of 16,000lb (7,300kg). The design service life was 6,000 flight hours, although this was later extended to 7,200 hours.[&#8202;](#ref-back-19-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-19)

Upon winning the contract for the F-14, Grumman greatly expanded its [Calverton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calverton,_New_York "Calverton, New York"), Long Island, New York facility for evaluating the aircraft. Much of the testing, including the first of many compressor stalls and multiple ejections, took place over Long Island Sound. To save time and avoid cancellation by the new presidential administration, the Navy skipped the prototype phase and jumped directly to full-scale development; the Air Force took a similar approach with its [McDonnell Douglas F-15 Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F-15_Eagle "McDonnell Douglas F-15 Eagle").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jenkins-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-Jenkins) The F-14 first flew on 21 December 1970, just 22 months after Grumman was awarded the contract. The fighter reached initial operational capability (IOC) in 1973. The [United States Marine Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Marine_Corps "United States Marine Corps") was initially interested in the F-14 as an F-4 Phantom II replacement, going so far as to send officers to [Fighter Squadron One Twenty-Four (VF-124)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-124 "VF-124") to train as instructors. The Marine Corps pulled out of any procurement when the development of the stores' management system for ground attack munitions was not pursued and decided the upcoming [F-18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F/A-18_Hornet "McDonnell Douglas F/A-18 Hornet") fit their role better.[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-21) An air-to-ground capability was not developed until the 1990s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jenkins-1)[\[19\]](#refnote-Jenkins)

Firing trials involved launches against simulated targets of various types, from cruise missiles to high-flying bombers. AIM-54 Phoenix missile testing from the F-14 began in April 1972. The longest single Phoenix launch was successful against a target at a range of 110nmi (200km) in April 1973. Another unusual test was made on 22 November 1973, when six missiles were fired within 38 seconds at Mach 0.78 and 24,800ft (7,600m); four scored direct hits, one broke the lock and missed, and one was declared "no test" after the radar signature augmentation in the target drone (which increased the apparent radar signature of the tiny drone to the size of a [MiG-21](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-21 "Mikoyan-Gurevich MiG-21")) failed, causing the missile to break track. This gave a tested success rate of 80% since effectively only 5 missiles were tested. This was the most expensive single test of air-to-air missiles ever performed at that time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-22)

### Improvements and changes

Throughout production, the F-14 underwent significant upgrades in missile armament, especially with the move to full solid-state electronics, primarily allowing for better [Electronic counter-countermeasures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_counter-countermeasure "Electronic counter-countermeasure") (ECCM) and more space for the rocket motor. The AIM-54A Phoenix [active-radar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_radar_homing "Active radar homing") air-to-air missile was upgraded with the AIM-54B (1983, limited use) and AIM-54C (1986) versions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-23) The initial AIM-7E-4[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj7p545-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-wapj7p545) Sparrow [semi-active radar homing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-active_radar_homing "Semi-active radar homing") was upgraded to the AIM-7F in 1976, and the M variant in 1982.[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-25) The [heat-seeking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_homing "Infrared homing") missile armament was upgraded from the AIM-9J/H to the joint Air Force/Navy missile, the AIM-9L in 1979, and then the AIM-9M in 1982.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj7p556-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-wapj7p556)

The [Tactical Airborne Reconnaissance Pod System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tactical_Airborne_Reconnaissance_Pod_System "Tactical Airborne Reconnaissance Pod System") (TARPS) was developed in the late 1970s for the F-14. Approximately 65 F-14As and all F-14Ds were modified to carry the pod.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Warplanes-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-Warplanes) TARPS was primarily controlled by the Radar Intercept Officer (RIO) via an extra display for observing reconnaissance data. The "TARPS Digital (TARPS-DI)" was a 1996 upgrade featuring a digital camera. The digital camera was further updated beginning in 1998 with the "TARPS Completely Digital (TARPS-CD)" configuration that also provided real-time transmission of imagery.[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-28)

In 1984, plans were announced to replace the existing TF30 engines of the Tomcat with [General Electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric "General Electric") [F110-GE-400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric_F110 "General Electric F110") turbofans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dorr_p.70-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-Dorr_p.70) An initial, interim, version just replaced the TF30 with the new engine, retaining the original avionics. These aircraft were designated F-14A+, which was changed to F-14B in May 1991. 38 F-14Bs were newly built, with a further 43 converted from F-14As.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj19p1324-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-wapj19p1324)[&#8202;](#ref-back-iapr3p52-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-iapr3p52) The F-14D variant was developed at the same time; it included the F110 engines with newer digital avionics systems such as a [glass cockpit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glass_cockpit "Glass cockpit") and compatibility with the [Link 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Link_16 "Link 16") secure datalink. 37 F-14Ds were new builds while another 18 were converted from F-14As.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-32) The Digital Flight Control System (DFCS) notably improved the F-14's handling qualities when flying at a high [angle of attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angle_of_attack "Angle of attack") or in [air combat maneuvering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_combat_manoeuvring "Air combat manoeuvring").[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-33)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/VF-143_F-14_F-18_2005.jpg/500px-VF-143_F-14_F-18_2005.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:VF-143_F-14_F-18_2005.jpg) VFA-143 "Pukin Dogs" F-14B (in the foreground) and F/A-18E Super Hornet in 2005

While the F-14 had been developed as a lightweight alternative to the 80,000lb (36,000kg) F-111B, the F-14 was still the heaviest and most expensive fighter of its time. VFAX was revived in the 1970s as a lower cost solution to replacing the Navy and Marine Corps' fleets of F-4s, and A-7s. VFAX was directed to review the fighters in the USAF [Light Weight Fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_Weight_Fighter "Light Weight Fighter") competition, which led to the development of the [McDonnell Douglas F/A-18 Hornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F/A-18_Hornet "McDonnell Douglas F/A-18 Hornet") as roughly a midsize fighter and attack aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Donald-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-Donald)

### Ground attack upgrades

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f3/F14Missile.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F14Missile.jpg) An F-14D launching an [AIM-7 Sparrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-7_Sparrow "AIM-7 Sparrow"); a [GBU-10 Paveway II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-10_Paveway_II "GBU-10 Paveway II") is also carried.

In the 1990s, with the pending retirement of the [Grumman A-6 Intruder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_A-6_Intruder "Grumman A-6 Intruder") and the cancellation of the [McDonnell Douglas A-12 Avenger II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_A-12_Avenger_II "McDonnell Douglas A-12 Avenger II") Advanced Tactical Aircraft (ATA), the F-14 [air-to-ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_attack_aircraft "Ground attack aircraft") program was resurrected. Trials with live bombs had been carried out in the 1980s; the F-14 was cleared to use basic [iron bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unguided_bomb "Unguided bomb") in 1992. During Operation Desert Storm of the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War "Gulf War"), most air-to-ground missions were left to [LTV A-7 Corsair II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LTV_A-7_Corsair_II "LTV A-7 Corsair II"), A-6 Intruder and F/A-18 Hornet squadrons, while the F-14s focused on air defense operations. Following Desert Storm, F-14As and F-14Bs underwent upgrades to avionics and cockpit displays to enable the use of precision munitions, enhance defensive systems, and apply structural improvements. The new avionics were comparable with the F-14D; these upgraded aircraft were designated F-14A (Upgrade) and F-14B (Upgrade) respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Warplanes-1)[\[26\]](#refnote-Warplanes)

By 1994, Grumman and the Navy were proposing ambitious plans for Tomcat upgrades beyond the D model (such as the Super Tomcat 21, the cheaper QuickStrike version, and the more advanced Attack Super Tomcat 21).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Donald-1)[\[33\]](#refnote-Donald) However, the upgrades would have taken too long to implement to meet the gap, and were priced in the billions. The [U.S. Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congress "United States Congress") considered this too expensive for an interim solution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Warplanes-2)[\[26\]](#refnote-Warplanes) A quick, inexpensive upgrade using the [Low Altitude Navigation and Targeting Infrared for Night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LANTIRN "LANTIRN") (LANTIRN) targeting pod was devised. The LANTIRN pod provided the F-14 with a forward-looking infrared (FLIR) camera for night operations and a laser target designator to direct laser-guided bombs (LGB).[&#8202;](#ref-back-iapr3p535-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-iapr3p535) Although LANTIRN is traditionally a two-pod system, an AN/AAQ-13 navigation pod with terrain-following radar and a wide-angle FLIR, along with an AN/AAQ-14 targeting pod with a steerable FLIR and a laser target designator, the decision was made to only use the targeting pod. The Tomcat's LANTIRN pod was altered and improved over the baseline configuration, such as a [Global Positioning System/Inertial Navigation System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GPS/INS "GPS/INS") (GPS/INS) capability to allow an F-14 to accurately locate itself. The pod was carried on the right wing glove pylon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-iapr3p535-1)[\[34\]](#refnote-iapr3p535)

[![Pale gray jet aircraft flying over water towards right, perpendicular to the camera. Horizon located two-thirds down the photo. Sky made up of two shades, dark blue covers the top, blending with a lighter shade until it is almost white above horizon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/US_Navy_051010-N-5088T-001_A_specially_painted_F-14D_Tomcat%2C_assigned_to_the_Blacklions_of_Fighter_Squadron_Two_One_Three_%28VF-213%29%2C_conducts_a_mission_over_the_Persian_Gulf.jpg/500px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:US_Navy_051010-N-5088T-001_A_specially_painted_F-14D_Tomcat,_assigned_to_the_Blacklions_of_Fighter_Squadron_Two_One_Three_(VF-213),_conducts_a_mission_over_the_Persian_Gulf.jpg) An F-14D(R) from [VF-213](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-213 "VF-213") flying over [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'athist_Iraq "Ba'athist Iraq") on the last Tomcat deployment with a LANTIRN pod on the starboard wing glove station and a [laser-guided bomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-12_Paveway_II "GBU-12 Paveway II") underneath the fuselage

The LANTIRN pod did not require changes to the F-14's own system software, but the pod was designed to operate on a MIL-STD-1553B bus not present on the F-14A or B. Consequently, [Martin Marietta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Marietta "Martin Marietta") specially developed an interface card for LANTIRN. The Radar Intercept Officer (RIO) would receive pod imagery on a 10-inch Programmable Tactical Information Display (PTID) or another Multi-Function Display in the F-14\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] rear cockpit and guided LGBs using a new hand controller installed on the right side console. Initially, the hand controller replaced the RIO's TARPS control panel, meaning a Tomcat configured for LANTIRN could not carry TARPS and the reverse, but eventually a workaround was later developed to allow a Tomcat to carry LANTIRN or TARPS as needed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-iapr3p535-2)[\[34\]](#refnote-iapr3p535)

An upgraded LANTIRN named "LANTIRN 40K" for operations up to 40,000ft (12,000m) was introduced in 2001, followed by Tomcat Tactical Targeting (T3) and Fast Tactical Imagery (FTI), to provide precise target coordinate determination and ability to transmit images in-flight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-USN_FF-1)[\[2\]](#refnote-USN_FF) Tomcats also added the ability to carry the [GBU-38](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-38 "GBU-38") Joint Direct Attack Munition (JDAM) in 2003, giving it the option of a variety of LGB and [GPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GPS "GPS")-guided weapons.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-14Ds_JDAM-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-F-14Ds_JDAM) Some F-14Ds were upgraded in 2005 with a [ROVER III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ROVER "ROVER") Full Motion Video (FMV) downlink, a system that transmits real-time images from the aircraft's sensors to the laptop of a [forward air controller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forward_air_control "Forward air control") (FAC) on the ground.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-14_ROVER-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-F-14_ROVER)

### Production termination

In the early 1980s, the Navy anticipated that an Advanced Carrier-Borne Multirole Fighter (VFMX) would eventually be the long term high-end replacement for both the F-14 and A-6, although this program was short-lived and was succeeded by separate efforts for fleet air defense and deep strike/attack; the latter would become the ATA program under which the A-12 Avenger II was being developed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-38)[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-39) In 1988, the service announced that it would procure a derivative of the Air Force's [Advanced Tactical Fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_Tactical_Fighter "Advanced Tactical Fighter") (ATF), named the Navy Advanced Tactical Fighter (NATF) that would eventually have been a [navalized variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_YF-22#NATF-22 "Lockheed YF-22") of the [F-22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-22_Raptor "Lockheed Martin F-22 Raptor"), as the long term F-14 replacement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-40) However, the collapse of the Soviet Union resulted in declining defense budgets and also in a steep increase in the unit cost of the ATF and NATF due to reduced production rates. With tightening budgets, the Navy chose to focus on its top aviation priority, the A-12, and canceled the NATF in 1991, believing that the F-14D with upgrades would be viable for fleet air defense until 2015. However, the A-12 was cancelled shortly afterwards due to cost overruns and technical problems. As a result, the Navy launched a new attack aircraft program, the Advanced-Attack (A-X), while the F-14 with LANTIRN and ground attack upgrades would provide some interim capabilities, with Grumman proposing even more F-14 upgrades in the 1990s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NATF_to_JSF-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-NATF_to_JSF)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/19/Lockheed-Boeing_F-22_NATF-22_A-X_AF-X.jpg/500px-Lockheed-Boeing_F-22_NATF-22_A-X_AF-X.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lockheed-Boeing_F-22_NATF-22_A-X_AF-X.jpg) From left to right, F-22, NATF, and Lockheed/Boeing A-X and A/F-X designs; the latter three were successive proposals for replacing the F-14.

Although the F-14D was to be the definitive version of the Tomcat, not all fleet units received the D variant. In 1989, Secretary of Defense [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney "Dick Cheney") refused to approve the purchase of any more F-14D model aircraft, stopping production after 37 F-14Ds had been built, although 18 more were produced by conversion of F-14As, giving a total of 55 F-14Ds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj19p138-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-wapj19p138) An upgrade to the F-14D's computer software to allow AIM-120 AMRAAM missile capability was planned but was later terminated to free up funding for LANTIRN integration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Warplanes-3)[\[26\]](#refnote-Warplanes) While upgrades kept the F-14 competitive with other teen series fighters, Cheney stated that the F-14 was 1960s technology. Despite an appeal from the Secretary of the Navy for at least 132 F-14Ds and some aggressive proposals from Grumman for a replacement,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jenkins_p48-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-Jenkins_p48) Cheney planned to replace the F-14 with a fighter that was not manufactured by Grumman. According to Cheney, the F-14 was a "jobs program", and when the F-14 was canceled, an estimated 80,000 jobs of Grumman employees, subcontractors, or support personnel were affected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-44) The Navy would end up procuring the [F/A-18E/F Super Hornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_F/A-18E/F_Super_Hornet "Boeing F/A-18E/F Super Hornet") as the interim strike fighter for the A-X, which had become A/F-X (Advanced Attack/Fighter) due to added fighter capabilities although A/F-X itself would be canceled in the 1993 Bottom-Up Review. Cheney's cancellation of the F-14D was controversial and contributed heavily to Grumman's decline and resulting acquisition by [Northrop Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Corporation "Northrop Corporation") to form [Northrop Grumman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Grumman "Northrop Grumman").[&#8202;](#ref-back-NATF_to_JSF-1)[\[40\]](#refnote-NATF_to_JSF)[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-45)

## Design

F-14 Tomcat flight demonstration video

The F-14 Tomcat was designed as both an air superiority fighter and a long-range naval interceptor,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spang_Background-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-Spang_Background)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spang_MemoVF2-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-Spang_MemoVF2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spang_VF10-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-Spang_VF10) which enabled it to both serve as escort fighter aircraft when armed with Sparrow missiles and [fleet air defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleet_air_defense "Fleet air defense") [loitering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loiter_(aeronautics) "Loiter (aeronautics)") interceptor role when armed with Phoenix missiles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution) The F-14 was designed with a two-seat cockpit with a [bubble canopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubble_canopy "Bubble canopy") which affords all-around visibility aiding aircrew in air-to-air combat. It features variable geometry wings that swing automatically during flight. For high-speed intercept, they are swept back and they swing forward for lower speed flight and increased endurance for loitering.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) It was designed to improve on the F-4 Phantom's air combat performance in most respects.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spang_Background-1)[\[45\]](#refnote-Spang_Background)

The F-14's fuselage and wings allow it to climb faster than the F-4, while the "twin-tail" [empennage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empennage "Empennage") (dual vertical stabilizers with ventral fins on the engine nacelles) offers better stability. The F-14 is equipped with an internal 20mm M61 Vulcan [rotary cannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotary_cannon "Rotary cannon") mounted on the left side (unlike the Phantom, which was not equipped with an internal gun in the US Navy), and can carry AIM-54 Phoenix, AIM-7 Sparrow, and AIM-9 Sidewinder anti-aircraft missiles. The twin engines are housed in widely spaced [nacelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podded_engine "Podded engine"). The flat area of the fuselage between the nacelles is used to contain fuel and avionics systems, such as the wing-sweep mechanism and flight controls, as well as weaponry since the wings are not used for carrying ordnance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-1)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) By itself, the [fuselage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuselage "Fuselage") provides approximately 40 to 60 percent of the F-14's aerodynamic lifting surface depending on the wing sweep position.[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-51) The [lifting body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifting_body "Lifting body") characteristics of the fuselage allowed one F-14 to safely land after suffering a [mid-air collision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-air_collision "Mid-air collision") that sheared off more than half of the plane's right wing in 1991.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-14_Tomcat_could_land_on_carrier_with_missing_radome,_damaged_wing-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-F-14_Tomcat_could_land_on_carrier_with_missing_radome,_damaged_wing)

The landing gear is very robust, in order to withstand [catapult launches (takeoffs) and recoveries (landings)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CATOBAR "CATOBAR") needed for carrier operations. It comprises a double nosewheel and widely spaced single main wheels. There are no hardpoints on the sweeping parts of the wings, and so all the [armament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armament "Armament") is fitted on the belly between the air [intake ramps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intake_ramp "Intake ramp") and on pylons under the wing gloves. Internal fuel capacity is 2,400USgal (9,100L): 290USgal (1,100L) in each wing, 690USgal (2,600L) in a series of tanks aft of the cockpit, and a further 457USgal (1,730L) in two feeder tanks. It can carry two 267USgal (1,010L) external [drop tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drop_tank "Drop tank") under the engine intake ramps.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-2)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) There is also an air-to-air refueling probe, which folds into the starboard nose.[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-53)

### Variable-geometry wings and aerodynamic design

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_SDASM.jpg/500px-Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_SDASM.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_SDASM.jpg) F-14 Tomcat with wings in asymmetric sweep during testing for this possible in-flight malfunction

The F-14's wing sweep can be varied between 20° and 68° in flight,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dorr_p.50.-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-Dorr_p.50.) and can be automatically controlled by its [Central Air Data Computer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-14_CADC "F-14 CADC") (CADC), which maintains wing sweep at the optimum [lift-to-drag ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lift-to-drag_ratio "Lift-to-drag ratio") as the [Mach number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mach_number "Mach number") varies; pilots can manually override the system if desired.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-3)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) When parked, the wings can be "overswept" to 75° to overlap the horizontal stabilizers to save deck space aboard carriers. In an emergency, the F-14 can land with the wings fully swept to 68°,[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-4)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) although this presents a significant safety hazard due to greatly increased stall speed. Such an aircraft would typically be diverted from an aircraft carrier to a land base if an incident did occur. The F-14 has flown safely with an asymmetrical wing-sweep during testing, and was deemed able to land aboard a carrier if needed in an emergency.[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-55)

The wing pivot points are significantly spaced far apart. This has two benefits. The first is that weaponry can be fitted on a pylon on the fixed wing glove, liberating the wings from having swiveling pylons fitted, a feature which had proven to add significant drag on the F-111B.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-1)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution) Since less of the total lifting area is variable, the center of lift moves less as the wings move, reducing trim drag at high speed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-2)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution) When the wing is swept back, its [thickness-to-chord ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thickness-to-chord_ratio "Thickness-to-chord ratio") decreases, which allows the aircraft to satisfy the Mach 2.4 top speed required by the U.S. Navy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-3)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution) The body of the aircraft contributes significantly to overall lift and so the Tomcat possesses a lower wing loading than its wing area would suggest. When carrying four Phoenix missiles or other heavy stores between the engines this advantage is lost and maneuverability is reduced in those configurations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-4)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution)

[![Rear view of stationary aircraft](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/F14_pancake.jpg/500px-F14_pancake.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hp_scan0021.jpg) Rear view of the F-14 showing the area between the engine nacelles

[Ailerons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aileron "Aileron") are not fitted, with [roll control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_dynamics_(aircraft) "Flight dynamics (aircraft)") being provided by wing-mounted [spoilers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spoiler_(aeronautics) "Spoiler (aeronautics)") at low speed (which are disabled if the sweep angle exceeds 57°), and by differential operation of the all-moving [tailerons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elevon "Elevon") at high speed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-5)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) Full-span [slats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leading_edge_slats "Leading edge slats") and [flaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flap_(aircraft) "Flap (aircraft)") are used to increase lift both for landing and combat, with slats being set at 17° for landing and 7° for combat, while flaps are set at 35° for landing and 10° for combat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-6)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) An air bag fills up the space occupied by the swept-back wing when the wing is in the forward position and a flexible fairing on top of the wing smooths out the shape transition between the fuselage and top wing area.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-5)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution) The twin tail layout helps in maneuvers at high angle of attack (AoA) while reducing the height of the aircraft to fit within the limited roof clearance of [hangars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangar "Hangar") aboard [aircraft carriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_carrier "Aircraft carrier").[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-7)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2)

The wings have a two-spar structure with integral fuel tanks. Around 25% of the structure is made of [titanium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium "Titanium"), including the wing box, wing pivots, and upper and lower wing skins;[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-8)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) this is a light, rigid, and strong material. [Electron beam welding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_beam_welding "Electron beam welding") was used in the construction of the titanium parts. The F-14 was designed for maneuver loads of 7.5g, but this was usually limited to 6.5g in the fleet to extend the aircraft's service life.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-6)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution)

Two triangular shaped retractable surfaces, called glove vanes, were originally mounted in the forward part of the wing glove, and could be automatically extended by the flight control system at high Mach numbers. They were used to generate additional [lift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lift_(force) "Lift (force)") ahead of the aircraft's [center of gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_of_gravity "Center of gravity"), thus helping to compensate for [mach tuck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mach_tuck "Mach tuck") at supersonic speeds. Automatically deployed at above Mach 1.4, they allowed the F-14 to pull 7.5 g at Mach 2 and could be manually extended with wings swept full aft. They were later disabled, however, owing to their additional weight and complexity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-9)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) The [air brakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_brake_(aircraft) "Air brake (aircraft)") consist of top-and-bottom extendable surfaces at the rearmost portion of the fuselage, between the engine nacelles. The bottom surface is split into left and right halves; the [tailhook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailhook "Tailhook") hangs between the two-halves, an arrangement sometimes called the "castor tail".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sgarlato_p40-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-Sgarlato_p40)

### Engines

The F-14A was initially equipped with two [Pratt & Whitney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney "Pratt & Whitney") TF30-P-412A (or JTF10A) augmented [turbofan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbofan "Turbofan") engines, each rated at 20,900lb (93kN) of static uninstalled thrust, which enabled the aircraft to attain a maximum speed of Mach 2.34.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spick_p81-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-Spick_p81) The F-14 would normally fly at a cruising speed for reduced [fuel consumption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuel_consumption "Fuel consumption"), which was important for conducting lengthy patrol missions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-58) The rectangular air inlets for the engines were equipped with movable ramps and bleed doors to meet the different airflow requirements of the engine from take-off to maximum supersonic speed. Variable nozzles were also fitted to the engine's exhaust. Late production F-14A had the improved TF30-P-414A engines. The Navy had originally planned to replace the TF30 with the Pratt & Whitney F401, the naval variant of the F-15's F100 engine and rated for 28,100lbs (125kN) of thrust, but this plan was ultimately canceled due to costs and reliability problems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj19p1301-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-wapj19p1301)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/F-14_Tomcat_preparing_to_refuel.jpg/500px-F-14_Tomcat_preparing_to_refuel.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14_Tomcat_preparing_to_refuel.jpg) An F-14D prepares to refuel with probe extended

The performance of the TF30 engine became an object of criticism. [John Lehman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lehman "John Lehman"), [Secretary of the Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy "United States Secretary of the Navy") in the 1980s, told the U.S. Congress that the TF30/F-14 combination was "probably the worst engine/airframe mismatch we have had in years" and that the TF30 was "a terrible engine";[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dorr_p.50.-1)[\[53\]](#refnote-Dorr_p.50.)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sgarlato_p40-1)[\[55\]](#refnote-Sgarlato_p40) 28% of all F-14 accidents were attributed to the engine. The TF30 was originally designed for the flight envelope of bomber applications, so in air combat they proved extremely susceptible to [compressor stalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compressor_stall "Compressor stall") especially at a high angle of attack and during rapid throttle transients or above 30,000ft (9,100m), which could easily result in loss of control, severe yaw oscillations, and could lead to an unrecoverable [flat spin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_spin_(aviation) "Flat spin (aviation)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-60) A high frequency of turbine [blade failures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbine_engine_failure "Turbine engine failure") led to the reinforcement of the entire engine bay to limit damage from such failures. At specific altitudes, exhaust produced by missile launches could cause an engine compressor [stall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stall_(engine) "Stall (engine)"). This led to the development of a bleed system that temporarily blocks the frontal intake ramp and reduces engine power during missile launch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-61)

The upgraded F-14A+, later redesignated F-14B, and F-14D were equipped with the General Electric F110-GE-400. The F110 provided a significant increase in thrust, with a static uninstalled thrust of 26,950lbf (120kN); installed thrust is 23,400lbf (104kN) with [afterburner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afterburner "Afterburner") at sea level, which rose to 30,200lbf (134kN) at Mach 0.9.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SAC_F-14D-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-SAC_F-14D)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NAVAIR-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-NAVAIR) The increased thrust gave the Tomcat a better than 1:1 thrust-to-weight ratio at low fuel quantities, and the rate of climb was increased by 61%. The basic engine thrust without afterburner was powerful enough for carrier launches. While this did result in fuel savings, the main reason not to use afterburner during carrier launches was that if an engine failed the F110's thrust in full afterburner would produce a yawing moment too abruptly for the pilot to correct. Thus the launch of an F-14B or F-14D with afterburner was rare, while the F-14A required full afterburner unless very lightly loaded. The F110 was also more efficient, allowing the Tomcat to cruise comfortably above 30,000ft (9,100m), which increased its range and survivability as well as endurance for time on station. In the overland attack role, this gave the F-14B and F-14D 60% more striking range or one-third more time on station.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-64) The F-14B arrived in time to participate in Desert Storm.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

With the TF30, the F-14's overall [thrust-to-weight ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust-to-weight_ratio "Thrust-to-weight ratio") at [maximum takeoff weight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_takeoff_weight "Maximum takeoff weight") is around 0.56, considerably less than the F-15A's ratio of 0.85; when fitted with the F110 engine, an improved thrust-to-weight ratio of 0.73 at maximum weight and 0.88 at normal takeoff weight was achieved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spick_p81-1)[\[56\]](#refnote-Spick_p81) Despite having large differences in static thrust, the TF30-equipped F-14A and the F110-equipped F-14B and F-14D were rated at the same top speed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[N 2\]](#refnote-65)[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-66)[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-67)

In 1996, two F110-equipped Tomcats crashed after an afterburner failure. In the second crash, lighting the afterburner damaged the afterburner can's lining and led to an explosion. The Navy prohibited the use of afterburner on the F-14A+/B/D below 10,000 feet until GE could redesign the afterburners, a process that took over a year to complete.[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-68)

### Avionics and flight controls

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/F-14_Tomcat_with_landing_gear_down.jpg/500px-F-14_Tomcat_with_landing_gear_down.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14_Tomcat_with_landing_gear_down.jpg) F-14 with landing gear deployed

The [cockpit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cockpit "Cockpit") has two seats, arranged in [tandem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tandem "Tandem"), outfitted with [Martin-Baker GRU-7A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin-Baker_Mk.7 "Martin-Baker Mk.7") rocket-propelled [ejection seats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ejection_seat "Ejection seat"), rated from zero altitude and zero airspeed up to 450 [knots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knot_(unit) "Knot (unit)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-69) The [canopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_canopy "Aircraft canopy") is spacious, and fitted with four mirrors to effectively provide all-round visibility. Only the pilot has [flight controls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_flight_control_system "Aircraft flight control system"); the flight instruments themselves are of a hybrid analog-digital nature.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-10)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) The cockpit also features a [head-up display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-up_display "Head-up display") (HUD) to show primarily navigational information; several other avionics systems such as communications and direction-finders are integrated into the AWG-9 radar's display. A feature of the F-14 is its [Central Air Data Computer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-14_CADC "F-14 CADC") (CADC), designed by [Garrett AiResearch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garrett_AiResearch "Garrett AiResearch"), that forms the onboard integrated flight control system. It uses a [MOSFET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOSFET "MOSFET")-based [large-scale integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large-scale_integration "Large-scale integration") [chipset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chipset "Chipset"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-70) specifically the MP944 chip.[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-71)

The aircraft's large nose contains a two-person crew and several bulky [avionics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avionics "Avionics") systems. The main element is the Hughes AN/AWG-9 [X band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X_band "X band") radar; the antenna is a 36in (91cm)-wide [planar array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planar_array "Planar array"), and has integrated [Identification friend or foe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identification_friend_or_foe "Identification friend or foe") antennas. The AWG-9 has several search and tracking modes, such as [track while scan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Track_while_scan "Track while scan") (TWS), range-while-search (RWS), [pulse-Doppler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulse-Doppler "Pulse-Doppler") single-target track (PDSTT), and jam angle track (JAT); a maximum of 24 targets can be tracked simultaneously, and six can be engaged in TWS mode up to around 60mi (97km). [Cruise missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruise_missile "Cruise missile") are also possible targets with the AWG-9, which [can lock onto and track small objects even at low altitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Look-down/shoot-down "Look-down/shoot-down") when in pulse-Doppler mode.[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-11)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) For the F-14D, the AWG-9 was replaced by the upgraded APG-71 radar. The [Joint Tactical Information Distribution System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Tactical_Information_Distribution_System "Joint Tactical Information Distribution System") (JTIDS)/Link 16 for data communications was added later on.[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-72)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_nose_detail.jpg/500px-Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_nose_detail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_nose_detail.jpg) F-14D AN/AAS-42 IRST and the TCS camera placed side-by-side under the nose

The F-14 also features [electronic countermeasures (ECM)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_countermeasure "Electronic countermeasure") and [radar warning receiver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_warning_receiver "Radar warning receiver") (RWR) systems, [chaff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaff_(radar_countermeasure) "Chaff (radar countermeasure)")/ [flare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flare_(countermeasure) "Flare (countermeasure)") dispensers, fighter-to-fighter data link, and a precise [inertial navigation system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inertial_navigation_system "Inertial navigation system").[&#8202;](#ref-back-baugher2-12)[\[49\]](#refnote-baugher2) The early navigation system was inertial-based; point-of-origin coordinates were programmed into a navigation computer and [gyroscopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyroscope "Gyroscope") would track the aircraft's every motion to calculate distance and direction from that starting point. [Global Positioning System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Positioning_System "Global Positioning System") later was integrated to provide more precise navigation and redundancy in case either system failed. The chaff/flare dispensers are located on the underside of the fuselage and on the tail. The F-14 was initially equipped with the AN/ALR-45/50 RWR system, while later production aircraft were equipped with the [AN/ALR-67](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AN/ALR-67 "AN/ALR-67"); the RWR system consists of several antennas on the aircraft's fuselage, which can roughly calculate both direction and distance of enemy radar users; it can also differentiate between search radar, tracking radar, and missile-homing radar.[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-73)

Featured in the sensor suite was the AN/ALR-23, an [infrared search and track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_search_and_track "Infrared search and track") (IRST) sensor using [indium antimonide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indium_antimonide "Indium antimonide") detectors, mounted under the nose; however the system was unreliable and was replaced by an optical system, Northrop's AAX-1, also designated TCS (TV Set). The AAX-1 helps pilots visually identify and track aircraft, up to a range of 60 miles (97km) for large aircraft\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]. The radar and the AAX-1 are linked, allowing the one detector to follow the direction of the other.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj19p1256-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-wapj19p1256) A dual infrared/optical detection system was adopted on the later F-14D, with the new AN/AAS-42 IRST and the TCS placed side-by-side.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj19p137-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-wapj19p137)

### Armament

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/F-14_carrying_AMRAAM.jpg/500px-F-14_carrying_AMRAAM.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14_carrying_AMRAAM.jpg) F-14 Tomcat carrying an [AIM-120 AMRAAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-120_AMRAAM "AIM-120 AMRAAM") during a 1982 test

The F-14 was designed to combat highly maneuverable aircraft as well as the Soviet anti-ship cruise missile and [bomber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bomber "Bomber") ( [Tupolev Tu-16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-16 "Tupolev Tu-16"), [Tupolev Tu-22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-22 "Tupolev Tu-22"), [Tupolev Tu-22M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-22M "Tupolev Tu-22M")) threats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spang_VF10-1)[\[47\]](#refnote-Spang_VF10) The Tomcat was to be a platform for the AIM-54 Phoenix, but unlike the canceled F-111B, it could also engage medium- and short-range threats with other weapons.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spang_Background-2)[\[45\]](#refnote-Spang_Background)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spang_VF10-2)[\[47\]](#refnote-Spang_VF10) The F-14 is an [air superiority fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_superiority_fighter "Air superiority fighter"), not just a long-range interceptor aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spang_VF10-3)[\[47\]](#refnote-Spang_VF10) Over 6,700kg (14,800lb) of stores can be carried for combat missions on several [hardpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoint "Hardpoint") under the fuselage and under the wing gloves. Commonly, this means a maximum of four Phoenixes or Sparrows on the belly stations, two Phoenixes/Sparrows on the wing hardpoints, and two Sidewinders on the wing glove hardpoints.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] The F-14 is also fitted with an internal 20mm M61 Vulcan rotary cannon. The Tomcat could also support MK-80 - MK-84 GBUs on its hardpoints. While in this configuration it was known to pilots as a "Bombcat".\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

Operationally, the capability to hold up to six Phoenix missiles was never used, although early testing was conducted; there was never a threat requirement to engage six hostile targets simultaneously and the load was too heavy to safely recover aboard an aircraft carrier in the event that the missiles were not fired. During the height of Cold War operations in the late 1970s and 1980s, the typical weapon loadout on carrier-deployed F-14s was usually two AIM-54 Phoenixes, augmented by two AIM-9 Sidewinders, three AIM-7 Sparrows, a full loadout of [20 mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_mm_caliber "20 mm caliber") ammunition and two drop tanks.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] The Phoenix missile was used twice in combat by the U.S. Navy, both over Iraq in 1999,[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-76)[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-77)[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-78) but the missiles did not score any kills.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] According to retired RIO Dave Baranek, the first two launch failures, on January 5, 1999, occurred when two F-14D Super Tomcats, carrying AIM-54Cs, fired two Phoenix missiles at a pair of MiG-23 jets. The missiles' rocket motors did not ignite because they were improperly armed prior to launch from the carrier.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-79)[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-80) However, as two F/A-18s chased the two MiG-23s, one MiG-23 ran out of fuel and crashed, killing the pilot. The US Navy did not claim a kill, but Captain James T. Knight, commander of CVW-11, said "Screw him...a kill is a kill."[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-81) On 14 September 1999, an F-14D assigned to CVW-2 aboard the [aircraft carrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_carrier "Aircraft carrier") [USS _Constellation_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Constellation_(CV-64) "USS Constellation (CV-64)") fired an AIM-54C missile at a MiG-23 at very long range. The MiG-23 quickly turned and fled, and was able to outrun the missile. Lieutenant Commander Coby "Coach" Loessberg, the Super Tomcat's pilot, commented afterward that had the Tomcat been closer to the center of the envelope, at optimal speed and altitude, a kill would have been more likely.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-82)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Irani_F-14_Tomcats_carrying_AIM-54_Phoenixs.jpg/500px-Irani_F-14_Tomcats_carrying_AIM-54_Phoenixs.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Irani_F-14_Tomcats_carrying_AIM-54_Phoenixs.jpg) Two Iranian Tomcats equipped with multiple missiles, c.1986, in the midst of a project to adapt [I-Hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-Hawk "I-Hawk") surface-to-air missiles for F-14s[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ward-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-Ward)

Iran made use of the Phoenix system, claiming [dozens of kills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Iranian_aerial_victories_during_the_Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_war "List of Iranian aerial victories during the Iran–Iraq war") with it during the 1980–1988 [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War "Iran–Iraq War"). Due to the shortage of air-to-air missiles as a result of sanctions, Iran tried to use other missiles on the Tomcat. It attempted to integrate the Russian [R-27R](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-27_(air-to-air_missile) "R-27 (air-to-air missile)") "Alamo" BVR missile, but was apparently unsuccessful.[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-84) In 1985, Iran started Project Sky Hawk, attempting to adapt [I-Hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-Hawk "I-Hawk") surface-to-air missiles, which Iran had in its inventory, for F-14s. The modified missiles were successfully tested in 1986 and one or two were used in combat, but the project was abandoned due to guidance problems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ward-1)[\[81\]](#refnote-Ward)

## Operational history

Main article: [F-14 Tomcat operational history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-14_Tomcat_operational_history "F-14 Tomcat operational history")

### United States

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Grumman_F-14A_Tomcat_of_VF-84_in_flight%2C_circa_in_1978.jpg/500px-Grumman_F-14A_Tomcat_of_VF-84_in_flight%2C_circa_in_1978.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14-vf-84.jpg) An F-14A of [VF-84 Jolly Rogers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-84_(1955-95) "VF-84 (1955-95)"), in a 1970s color scheme, circa 1978

The F-14 began replacing the F-4 Phantom II in U.S. Navy service starting in September 1974 with squadrons [VF-1 "Wolfpack"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-1 "VF-1") and [VF-2 "Bounty Hunters"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-2 "VFA-2") aboard [USS _Enterprise_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Enterprise_(CVN-65) "USS Enterprise (CVN-65)") and participated in the [American withdrawal from Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Frequent_Wind "Operation Frequent Wind"). The F-14 had its first kills in U.S. Navy service on 19 August 1981 over the [Gulf of Sidra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Sidra "Gulf of Sidra") in what is known as the [Gulf of Sidra incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Sidra_incident_(1981) "Gulf of Sidra incident (1981)"). In that engagement, two F-14s from [VF-41 Black Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-41 "VFA-41") were engaged by two Libyan [Su-22 "Fitters"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-17 "Sukhoi Su-17"). The F-14s evaded the Libyan missile and returned fire, downing both Libyan aircraft with AIM-9L Sidewinders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj7p745-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-wapj7p745) U.S. Navy F-14s once again were pitted against Libyan aircraft on 4 January 1989, when two F-14s from [VF-32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-32 "VFA-32") shot down two Libyan [MiG-23 "Floggers"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-23 "Mikoyan-Gurevich MiG-23") over the Gulf of Sidra in a second [Gulf of Sidra incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Sidra_incident_(1989) "Gulf of Sidra incident (1989)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj7p767-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-wapj7p767)

Its first sustained combat use was as a photo reconnaissance platform. The Tomcat was selected to inherit the reconnaissance mission upon the departure of the dedicated [North American RA-5C Vigilante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_A-5_Vigilante "North American A-5 Vigilante") and [Vought RF-8G Crusaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vought_F-8_Crusader "Vought F-8 Crusader") from the fleet. A large pod called the Tactical Airborne Reconnaissance Pod System (TARPS) was developed and fielded on the Tomcat in 1981. With the retirement of the last RF-8G Crusaders in 1982, TARPS F-14s became the U.S. Navy's primary tactical reconnaissance system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-87) One of two Tomcat squadrons per airwing was designated as a TARPS unit and received 3 TARPS capable aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj9p57-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-wapj9p57)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/F-14_Tomcat_VF-114_escorting_TU-95_Bear.jpg/500px-F-14_Tomcat_VF-114_escorting_TU-95_Bear.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14_Tomcat_VF-114_escorting_TU-95_Bear.jpg) An F-14A from [VF-114](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-114 "VF-114") intercepting a Soviet [Tu-95RT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-95 "Tupolev Tu-95") "Bear-D" maritime reconnaissance aircraft

While the Tomcat was being used by Iran in combat against Iraq in its intended air superiority mission in the early 1980s, the U.S. Navy found itself flying regular daily combat missions over Lebanon to photograph activity in the [Bekaa Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bekaa_Valley "Bekaa Valley"). At the time, the Tomcat had been thought too large and vulnerable to be used over land, but the need for imagery was so great that Tomcat aircrews developed high-speed medium altitude tactics to deal with considerable AAA and [SA-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SA-7 "SA-7") SAM threat in the Bekaa area. The first exposure of a Navy Tomcat to an [SA-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-75_Dvina "S-75 Dvina") missile was over Somalia in April 1983 when a local battery was unaware of two Tomcats scheduled for a TARPS mission in a prelude to an upcoming international exercise in the vicinity of Berbera. An SA-2 was fired at the second Tomcat while conducting 10,000ft (3,000m) mapping profile at max conserve setting. The Tomcat aircrews spotted the missile launch and dove for the deck thereby evading it without damage. The unexpected demand for combat TARPS laid the way for high altitude sensors such as the KA-93 Long Range Optics (LOROP) to be rapidly procured for the Tomcat as well as an Expanded Chaff Adapter (ECA) to be incorporated in an AIM-54 Phoenix Rail. Commercial "fuzz buster" type radar detectors were also procured and mounted in pairs in the forward cockpit as a stop gap solution to detect SAM radars such as the SA-6. The ultimate solution was an upgrade to the ALR-67 then being developed, but it would not be ready until the advent of the F-14A+ later in the 1980s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-89)

[![Portrait photography of four aircraft overflying orange desert and almost-flat terrain; horizon is blurred. Leading is black aircraft, followed by two single-engine jet aircraft, the one closer to camera being refueled by leading jet via a stiff hose connecting the two. Closest jet to camera is pale gray, has two engines and vertical fins, flying with wings unswept.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/7/75/Gypsy_escortDN-ST-91-05966.jpg/500px-Gypsy_escortDN-ST-91-05966.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gypsy_escortDN-ST-91-05966.jpg) An F-14A of [VF-32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-32 "VF-32") during [Operation Desert Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Desert_Storm "Operation Desert Storm") with a [KC-135 Stratotanker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KC-135_Stratotanker "KC-135 Stratotanker") and two [EA-6B Prowlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EA-6B_Prowler "EA-6B Prowler") in the background

The participation of the F-14 in the 1991 Operation Desert Storm consisted of Combat Air Patrol (CAP) over the Red Sea and the Persian Gulf and overland missions consisting of strike escort and reconnaissance. Until the waning days of Desert Storm, in-country air superiority was tasked to USAF F-15 Eagles due to the way the Air Tasking Orders (ATO) delegated primary overland CAP stations to the F-15. The governing Rules of Engagement (ROE) also dictated a strict Identification Friend or Foe (IFF) requirement when employing Beyond Visual Range weapons such as the AIM-7 Sparrow and particularly the AIM-54 Phoenix. This hampered the Tomcat from using its most powerful weapon. Furthermore, the powerful emissions from the AWG-9 radar are detectable at great range with a radar warning receiver. Iraqi fighters routinely retreated as soon as the Tomcats "lit them up" with the AWG-9.[&#8202;](#ref-back-gillcrest1-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-gillcrest1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-snodgrassinterview-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-snodgrassinterview) The U.S. Navy suffered its only F-14 loss from enemy action on 21 January 1991 when BuNo 161430, an F-14A upgraded to an F-14A+, from [VF-103](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-103 "VFA-103") was shot down by an SA-2 surface-to-air missile while on an escort mission near [Al Asad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Asad "Al Asad") airbase in Iraq. Both crew members survived ejection with the pilot being rescued by USAF Special Operation Forces and the RIO being captured by Iraqi troops as a POW until the end of the war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-92) An aircraft from [VF-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fighter_Squadron_1_(United_States_Navy) "Fighter Squadron 1 (United States Navy)") also achieved the F-14's final kill in US service, a [Mi-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mil_Mi-8 "Mil Mi-8") "Hip" helicopter, with an AIM-9 Sidewinder.[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-93)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/F-14D_with_GBU-10_over_Afghanistan_2001.JPEG/500px-F-14D_with_GBU-10_over_Afghanistan_2001.JPEG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14D_with_GBU-10_over_Afghanistan_2001.JPEG) A [Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Navy "U.S. Navy") F-14D flying over the skies of Afghanistan on a [precision bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precision_bombing "Precision bombing") mission [in November 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Afghanistan "United States invasion of Afghanistan")

In 1995, F-14s from [VF-14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-14 "VFA-14") and VF-41 participated in [Operation Deliberate Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Deliberate_Force "Operation Deliberate Force") as well as [Operation Allied Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Allied_Force "Operation Allied Force") in 1999, and in 1998, VF-32 and VF-213 participated in Operation Desert Fox. On 15 February 2001, the [Joint Direct Attack Munition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Direct_Attack_Munition "Joint Direct Attack Munition") (JDAM) was added to the Tomcat's arsenal. On 7 October 2001, F-14s would lead some of the first strikes into [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan "Afghanistan") marking the start of [Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom "Operation Enduring Freedom") and the first F-14 drop of a JDAM occurred on 11 March 2002. F-14s from VF-2, [VF-31](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-31 "VF-31"), [VF-32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-32 "VF-32"), [VF-154](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-154 "VF-154"), and [VF-213](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-213 "VF-213") would also participate in [Operation Iraqi Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Iraqi_Freedom "Operation Iraqi Freedom"). The F-14Ds of VF-2, VF-31, and VF-213 obtained JDAM capability in March 2003.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-14Ds_JDAM-1)[\[35\]](#refnote-F-14Ds_JDAM) On 10 December 2005, the F-14Ds of VF-31 and VF-213 were upgraded with a ROVER III downlink for transmitting images to a ground Forward Air Controller (FAC).[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-14_ROVER-1)[\[36\]](#refnote-F-14_ROVER) The Navy decided to retire the F-14 with the F/A-18E/F Super Hornet filling the roles of fleet defense and strike formerly filled by the F-14.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-94)[&#8202;](#ref-back-mkfflt-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-mkfflt)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/F-14_final_catapult_July_2006.jpg/500px-F-14_final_catapult_July_2006.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14_final_catapult_July_2006.jpg) The last F-14 launch from a carrier, USS _Theodore Roosevelt_, on 28 July 2006

The last American F-14 combat mission was completed on 8 February 2006, when a pair of Tomcats landed aboard [USS _Theodore Roosevelt_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Theodore_Roosevelt_(CVN-71) "USS Theodore Roosevelt (CVN-71)") after one dropped a bomb over [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq "Iraq"). During their final deployment with _Theodore Roosevelt_, VF-31 and VF-213 collectively completed 1,163 combat sorties totaling 6,876 flight hours, and dropped 9,500lb (4,300kg) of ordnance during reconnaissance, surveillance, and close air support missions in support of Operation Iraqi Freedom.[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-96) USS _Theodore Roosevelt_ launched an F-14D, of VF-31, for the last time on 28 July 2006; piloted by Lt. Blake Coleman and Lt. Cmdr Dave Lauderbaugh as RIO.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-97) The last two F-14 squadrons, the VF-31 Tomcatters and the VF-213 Black Lions conducted their last fly-in at [Naval Air Station Oceana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Oceana "Naval Air Station Oceana") on 10 March 2006.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-98)

The official final flight retirement ceremony was on 22 September 2006 at Naval Air Station Oceana and was flown by Lt. Cmdr. Chris Richard and Lt. Mike Petronis as RIO in a backup F-14 after the primary aircraft experienced mechanical problems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pilot-Online-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-Pilot-Online)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Vanden_Brook-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-Vanden_Brook) The actual last flight of an F-14 in U.S. service took place 4 October 2006, when an F-14D of VF-31 was ferried from NAS Oceana to [Republic Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_Airport "Republic Airport") on Long Island, New York.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Vanden_Brook-1)[\[98\]](#refnote-Vanden_Brook) The remaining intact F-14 aircraft in the U.S. were flown to and stored at the [309th Aerospace Maintenance and Regeneration Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/309th_Aerospace_Maintenance_and_Regeneration_Group "309th Aerospace Maintenance and Regeneration Group") "Boneyard", at [Davis-Monthan Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davis-Monthan_Air_Force_Base "Davis-Monthan Air Force Base"), Arizona; in 2007 the U.S. Navy announced plans to shred the remaining F-14s to prevent any components from being acquired by Iran.[&#8202;](#ref-back-shred-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-shred) In August 2009, the 309th AMARG stated that the last aircraft were taken to HVF West, [Tucson, Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucson,_Arizona "Tucson, Arizona") for shredding. At that time only 11 F-14s remained in desert storage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-amarg-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-amarg)

On 28 April 2026, the [U.S. Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate "United States Senate") approved the "Maverick Act" bill, authored by U.S. Rep. Abraham Hamadeh[&#8202;](#ref-back-MaverickAct1-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-MaverickAct1) and sponsored by Sen. Tim Sheehy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MaverickAct2-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-MaverickAct2) The bill proposes to authorize the [secretary of the Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy "United States Secretary of the Navy") (SECNAV) to transfer three F-14Ds currently in the Navy's inventory to the [U.S. Space & Rocket Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Space_&_Rocket_Center "U.S. Space & Rocket Center") in Huntsville, Alabama, for restoration to flyable status, enabling their usage in airshows. The bill has yet to be approved as a law as of May 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MaverickAct1-1)[\[101\]](#refnote-MaverickAct1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-MaverickAct2-1)[\[102\]](#refnote-MaverickAct2)

### Iran

See also: [Air force history of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_force_history_of_Iran "Air force history of Iran") and [Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_of_Iran_Air_Force "Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force")

[![Iranian F-14 Tomcat](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Iranian_Air_Force_exhibition_%2820%29.jpg/500px-Iranian_Air_Force_exhibition_%2820%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Iranian_Air_Force_exhibition_(20).jpg) Iranian F-14 Tomcat

Although attempts had been made to sell the Tomcat to the air forces of [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Air_Force "Royal Canadian Air Force"), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Air_Force "German Air Force"), and [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Air_Self-Defense_Force "Japan Air Self-Defense Force"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-105) the [Imperial Iranian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_force_history_of_Iran "Air force history of Iran") (IIAF) would ultimately be the sole foreign customer for the Tomcat.
