During the reign of the last [Shah of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_of_Iran "Shah of Iran"), [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Reza_Pahlavi "Mohammad Reza Pahlavi"), in the early 1970s, the IIAF was searching for an advanced fighter, specifically one capable of intercepting Soviet [MiG-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-25 "Mikoyan-Gurevich MiG-25") reconnaissance flights. After a visit of U.S. President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon "Richard Nixon") to [Pahlavi Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran "Pahlavi Iran") in 1972, during which Iran was offered the latest in American military technology, the IIAF selected and initiated acquisition of the F-14 Tomcat, but offered [McDonnell Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas "McDonnell Douglas") the chance to demonstrate its [F-15 Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_F-15_Eagle "McDonnell Douglas F-15 Eagle").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Iranian_F-14_Tomcat_Units_in_Combat-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-Iranian_F-14_Tomcat_Units_in_Combat) The US Navy and Grumman Corporation arranged competitive demonstrations of the Eagle and the Tomcat at [Andrews AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrews_AFB "Andrews AFB") for the Shah and high-ranking officers, and in January 1974 Iran placed an order for 30 F-14s and 424 [AIM-54 Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-54_Phoenix "AIM-54 Phoenix") missiles, initiating Project _Persian King_, worth US$300million. A few months later, this order was increased to a total of 80 Tomcats and 714 Phoenix missiles as well as spare parts and replacement engines for 10 years, complete armament package, and support infrastructure (including construction of the [Khatami Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan_International_Airport "Isfahan International Airport") near [Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan "Isfahan")).[&#8202;](#ref-back-f14iran-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-f14iran):25

The first F-14 arrived in January 1976, modified only by the removal of classified avionics components, but fitted with the TF30-414 engines. The following year 12 more were delivered. Meanwhile, training of the first groups of Iranian crews by the U.S. Navy was underway in the US; one of these conducted a successful shoot-down with a Phoenix missile of a [target drone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Target_drone "Target drone") flying at 50,000ft (15km).[&#8202;](#ref-back-f14iran-1)[\[105\]](#refnote-f14iran):11

Following the [overthrow of the Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Revolution "Iranian Revolution") in 1979, the air force was renamed the [Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_of_Iran_Air_Force "Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force") (IRIAF) and the post-revolution [Interim Government of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_Government_of_Iran_(1979) "Interim Government of Iran (1979)") canceled most Western arms orders. In 1980, an Iranian F-14 shot down an Iraqi [Mil Mi-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mil_Mi-24 "Mil Mi-24") helicopter for its first air-to-air kill during the Iran–Iraq War (1980–1988).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Persian_Cats-2)[\[3\]](#refnote-Persian_Cats) According to research by [Tom Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Cooper_(military_historian) "Tom Cooper (military historian)"), Iranian F-14s scored at least 50 [air-to-air victories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Iranian_aerial_victories_during_the_Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_war "List of Iranian aerial victories during the Iran–Iraq war") in the first six months of the war against Iraqi [MiG-21s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-21 "Mikoyan-Gurevich MiG-21"), [MiG-23s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-23 "MiG-23") and some [Su-20s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-20 "Su-20")/ [22s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-22 "Su-22"). During the same period, only one Iranian F-14 suffered damage after being hit by debris from a nearby MiG-21 that exploded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Persian_Cats_–_acig.org-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-Persian_Cats_–_acig.org)

Iranian Tomcats were originally used as an early-warning platform assisting other less-sophisticated aircraft with targeting and defense. They were also crucial to the defense of areas deemed vital by the Iranian government, such as oil terminals on [Kharg Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharg_Island "Kharg Island") and industrial infrastructure in the capital [Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran "Tehran"). Many of these patrols had the support of [Boeing 707-3J9C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_707#707-320C "Boeing 707") and [747-131(SF)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_747#Government,_military,_and_other_variants "Boeing 747") in-flight refueling tankers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-109) As fighting escalated between 1982 and 1986, the F-14s gradually became more involved in the battle. They performed well, but their primary role was to intimidate the [Iraqi Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Air_Force "Iraqi Air Force") and avoid heavy engagement to protect the fleet's numbers. Their presence was often enough to drive away opposing Iraqi fighters. The precision and effectiveness of the Tomcat's AWG-9 weapons system and AIM-54A Phoenix long-range air-to-air missiles enabled the F-14 to maintain air superiority.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Persian_Cats_–_acig.org-1)[\[106\]](#refnote-Persian_Cats_–_acig.org)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Iran_–_National_Interest-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-Iran_–_National_Interest) In December 1980, an Iraqi [MiG-21bis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-21bis "MiG-21bis") accounted for the only confirmed kill of an F-14 by that type of aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-auto) On 11 August 1984, a [MiG-23ML](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-23ML "MiG-23ML") shot down an F-14A using an [R-60 missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-60_(missile) "R-60 (missile)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-1)[\[109\]](#refnote-auto) On 2 September 1986, a MiG-23ML using an [R-24T missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-23_(missile) "R-23 (missile)") mistakenly shot down an F-14 that was defecting to Iraq.[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-112) On 17 January 1987, another Iranian F-14A was shot down; according to some sources it was shot down by a MiG-23ML.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-2)[\[109\]](#refnote-auto) According to the latest data, the F-14A, which was shot down on 17 January, was destroyed by an [R-40 missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-40_(missile) "R-40 (missile)") fired by an Iraqi MiG-25PDS (pilot Captain Adnan Sae’ed), and the MiG-23 pilot did not claim any victory.[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-113)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Iranian_F-14_Pilots.jpg/500px-Iranian_F-14_Pilots.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Iranian_F-14_Pilots.jpg) Iranian F-14 pilots standing in front of an Imperial Iranian Air Force F-14

Iraq also obtained [Mirage F.1EQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_F1 "Dassault Mirage F1") fighters from France in 1981, armed with [Super 530F](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_530 "Super 530") and [Magic Mk.2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R.550_Magic "R.550 Magic") air-to-air missiles. The Mirage F.1 fighters were eventually responsible for four confirmed F-14 kills.[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-114) The IRIAF attempted to keep 60 F-14s operational throughout the war, but reports indicate this number was reduced to 30 by 1986 with only half fully mission-capable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Persian_Cats_–_acig.org-2)[\[106\]](#refnote-Persian_Cats_–_acig.org)[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-115)

Based on research by Tom Cooper and Farzad Bishop, Iran claimed their F-14s shot down at least 160 Iraqi aircraft during the Iran–Iraq War, including 58 [MiG-23s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan-Gurevich_MiG-23 "Mikoyan-Gurevich MiG-23") (15 of these are confirmed according to Cooper), 33 [Mirage F1s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirage_F1 "Mirage F1"), 23 [MiG-21s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-21 "MiG-21"), 23 [Su-20s/22s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-17 "Sukhoi Su-17"), nine [MiG-25s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-25 "MiG-25") (one of these are confirmed according to Iraqi sources), five [Tu-22s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tu-22 "Tu-22"), two [MiG-27s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-27 "Mikoyan MiG-27"), one [Mil Mi-24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mil_Mi-24 "Mil Mi-24"), one [Dassault Mirage 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_5 "Dassault Mirage 5"), one [B-6D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xian_H-6 "Xian H-6"), one [Aérospatiale Super Frelon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C3%A9rospatiale_SA_321_Super_Frelon "Aérospatiale SA 321 Super Frelon"), and two unidentified aircraft. Despite the circumstances the F-14s and their crews faced during the war against Iraq – lacking support from [AWACS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airborne_early_warning_and_control "Airborne early warning and control"), [AEW aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airborne_early_warning_aircraft "List of airborne early warning aircraft"), and [Ground Control Intercept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground-controlled_interception "Ground-controlled interception") (GCI) – the F-14 proved to be successful in combat. It achieved this in the midst of a confrontation with an enemy that was constantly upgrading its capabilities and receiving support from three major countries – France, the US, and the USSR. Part of the success is attributed to the resilient [Iranian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_economy "Iranian economy") and IRIAF personnel.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Persian_Cats-3)[\[3\]](#refnote-Persian_Cats)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Persian_Cats_–_acig.org-3)[\[106\]](#refnote-Persian_Cats_–_acig.org)

While Iraq's army claimed it shot down more than 70 F-14s, the [Foreign Broadcast Information System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Broadcast_Information_System "Foreign Broadcast Information System") in Washington DC estimated that Iran lost 12 to 16 F-14s during the war. Cooper writes three F-14s were shot down by Iraqi pilots and four by Iranian [surface-to-air missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface-to-air_missile "Surface-to-air missile") (SAM). Two more Tomcats were lost in unknown circumstances during the battle, and seven crashed due to technical failure or accidents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-116) During the war, the Iranian Air Force F-14s suffered ten confirmed losses, one lost due to engine stall, one in unknown conditions, two by [Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friendly_fire "Friendly fire") [HAWK SAMs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIM-23_Hawk "MIM-23 Hawk"), two by MIG-23s and four were shot down by Mirage F-1s. There are also unconfirmed reports of the downing of 10 more Tomcats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-117)

On 31 August 1986, an Iranian F-14A armed with at least one AIM-54A missile defected to [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'athist_Iraq "Ba'athist Iraq"). Then again on 2 September 1986 another Iranian F-14A defected to Iraq.[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-118)[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-119)

On 24 July 2002, an Iranian F-14A confronted two [Azerbaijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan "Azerbaijan") MiG-25s that were threatening an Iranian [P-3F](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_P-3_Orion "Lockheed P-3 Orion"), securing a radar lock on one of the MiGs, which then turned away, during tensions over attempts by Azerbaijan to survey for oil in Iranian waters in the [Caspian Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caspian_Sea "Caspian Sea").[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-120)

Iran had an estimated 44 F-14s in 2009 according to Combat Aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Combat_Aircraft-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-Combat_Aircraft) _[Aviation Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aviation_Week_&_Space_Technology "Aviation Week & Space Technology")_ estimated it had 19 operational F-14s in January 2013,[&#8202;](#ref-back-2013_Aero_Source-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-2013_Aero_Source) and [FlightGlobal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FlightGlobal "FlightGlobal") estimated that 28 were in service in 2014.[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-123)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/A_formation_of_Iranian_Tomcats_in_flight.jpg/500px-A_formation_of_Iranian_Tomcats_in_flight.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:A_formation_of_Iranian_Tomcats_in_flight.jpg) Formation flight of Iranian Tomcats, 2008

Following the US Navy's retirement of its Tomcats in 2006, Iran sought to purchase spare parts for its aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ai918p58-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-ai918p58) In January 2007, the [U.S. Department of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_Defense "U.S. Department of Defense") announced that sales of spare F-14 parts would be suspended over concerns of the parts ending up in Iran.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-125) In July 2007, the remaining American F-14s were shredded to ensure that any parts could not be acquired.[&#8202;](#ref-back-shred-1)[\[99\]](#refnote-shred) Despite these measures, Iran managed to significantly increase its stocks of spare parts, increasing the number of airworthy Tomcats, although it did not manage to obtain spare parts for the aircraft's weapon systems, the number of combat ready Tomcats was still low (seven in 2008).[&#8202;](#ref-back-ai918p58-1)[\[124\]](#refnote-ai918p58) In 2010, Iran requested that the U.S. deliver the 80th F-14 that it had purchased in 1974 but never received due to the Islamic Revolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-127)[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-128) In October 2010, an Iranian Air Force commander claimed that the country overhauls and optimizes different types of military aircraft, mentioning their Air Force has installed Iran-made radar systems on the F-14.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-129) In 2012, the Iranian Air Force's Mehrabad Overhaul Center delivered an F-14 with upgraded weapon systems with locally sourced components, designated F-14AM.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ai918p59-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-ai918p59) Shortages of Phoenix missiles led to attempts to integrate the Russian [R-27 semi-active radar-guided missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-27_(air-to-air_missile) "R-27 (air-to-air missile)"), but these proved unsuccessful. An alternative was the use of modified [MIM-23 Hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIM-23_Hawk "MIM-23 Hawk") missiles to replace the Tomcat's Phoenixes and Sparrows, but as the Tomcat could only carry two Hawks, this project was also abandoned, and the [Fakour-90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fakour-90 "Fakour-90") missile, which used the guidance system of the Hawk packaged into the airframe of the Phoenix, launched. Pre-production Fakour-90s were delivered in 2017, and a production order for 100 missiles (now designated AIM-23B) was placed in 2018, intending to replace the F-14s AIM-7E Sparrow missiles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ai918p61-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-ai918p61)

On 26 January 2012, an Iranian F-14 scrambled to intercept an intruder flying towards the Bushehr nuclear power plant,[&#8202;](#ref-back-forbes-peck-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-forbes-peck) but crashed three minutes after takeoff. Both crew members were killed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-133) No cause for the incident has been determined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-forbes-peck-1)[\[130\]](#refnote-forbes-peck)

In November 2015, Iranian F-14s were reported flying escort for Russian [Tu-95](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-95 "Tupolev Tu-95"), [Tu-160](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-160 "Tupolev Tu-160") and [Tu-22M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-22M "Tupolev Tu-22M") bombers on [air strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_military_intervention_in_the_Syrian_Civil_War "Russian military intervention in the Syrian Civil War") in Syria against the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant "Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant").[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-134)[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-135)

On 14 May 2019, an Iranian F-14 crashed during landing at Isfahan-Shahid Beheshti Airport. Both crew members ejected and survived.[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-136)\[ _[needs update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_\]

On 16 June 2025, the [Israel Defense Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Defense_Forces "Israel Defense Forces") released video footage showing a pair of F-14s were destroyed in [air strikes against Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War "Twelve-Day War").[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-137) On 21 June 2025, the IDF released video footage showing another three F-14s being destroyed by airstrikes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TOI_3_F-14_Destroyed-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-TOI_3_F-14_Destroyed)

According to [Tom Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Cooper_(military_historian) "Tom Cooper (military historian)"), prior to the [2026 Iran war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Iran_war "2026 Iran war") the IRIAF had a total of 10 operational Tomcats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NPR-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-NPR) On 9 March 2026 satellite imagery showed at least 8 destroyed F-14s in [Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan "Isfahan")[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-140)[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-141) as a result of [Israeli airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Roaring_Lion "Operation Roaring Lion"); according to Cooper, some of these aircraft were actually wooden decoys.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NPR-1)[\[137\]](#refnote-NPR) It is unknown if there are any flight-worthy F-14s as of September 2026.

### Notable F-14 crew members

#### Iran

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Jalil_Z.jpg/500px-Jalil_Z.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jalil_Z.jpg) Iranian ace [Jalil Zandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalil_Zandi "Jalil Zandi") is credited with shooting down eleven Iraqi aircraft during the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War "Iran–Iraq War"), making him the highest-scoring F-14 pilot in history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Combat_2004,_pp._85-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-Combat_2004,_pp._85)

- [Assadollah Adeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assadollah_Adeli "Assadollah Adeli") – IRIAF ace pilot credited with five aerial victories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-razoux2-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-razoux2)
- [Fereidoun Ali-Mazandarani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fereidoun_Ali-Mazandarani "Fereidoun Ali-Mazandarani") – IRIAF ace pilot with an estimated nine[&#8202;](#ref-back-razoux1-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-razoux1) to eleven[&#8202;](#ref-back-razoux2-1)[\[141\]](#refnote-razoux2) victories.
- [Fazlollah Javidnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazlollah_Javidnia "Fazlollah Javidnia") – IRIAF ace pilot with eleven confirmed and two probable victories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-razoux2-2)[\[141\]](#refnote-razoux2)
- [Jalil Zandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalil_Zandi "Jalil Zandi") – IRIAF ace pilot and highest-scoring F-14 pilot. Credited with eleven confirmed kills during the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War "Iran–Iraq War").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Combat_2004,_pp._85-1)[\[140\]](#refnote-Combat_2004,_pp._85)

#### United States

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/SnodgrassBananaPass.jpg/500px-SnodgrassBananaPass.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SnodgrassBananaPass.jpg) Dale "Snort" Snodgrass performing the "banana pass" stunt over the [USS _America_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_America_(CV-66) "USS America (CV-66)") in 1988

- [Scott Altman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Altman "Scott Altman") – former [NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA "NASA") [astronaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronaut "Astronaut") and commander of Space Shuttle missions [STS-109](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-109 "STS-109") (2002) and [STS-125](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-125 "STS-125") (2009). Altman deployed twice on the [USS _Carl Vinson_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Carl_Vinson "USS Carl Vinson") as an F-14 pilot of [VF-51](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-51 "VF-51") from 1984 to 1987 and worked as a test pilot for F-14 test programs, including the first deployment of the F-14D variant in 1992, before being selected for the NASA astronaut program. He also participated in the filming of _Top Gun_ as a stunt pilot, most notably the tower flyby stunt.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TomcastTopGun-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-TomcastTopGun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-146)
- [Walter E. Carter Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_E._Carter_Jr. "Walter E. Carter Jr.") – retired Navy vice admiral, 62nd superintendent of the U.S. Naval Academy, and former president of the [Ohio State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_State_University "Ohio State University").[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-147) Carter accumulated 6,150 flight hours and 2,016 carrier-arrested landings as a RIO on the F-4, F-14, and F/A-18. He transitioned to the F-14 in 1986 through [VF-124](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-124 "VF-124") in NAS Miramar while simultaneously taking instructor duty and subsequently deployed with [VF-21](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-21 "VF-21") on the [USS _Independence_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Independence_(CV-62) "USS Independence (CV-62)") during the Gulf War. He later assumed command of VF-14 in 1998 and led the squadron in the completion of 550 combat missions during [Operation Allied Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Allied_Force "Operation Allied Force").[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-148)
- [Donnie Cochran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donnie_Cochran "Donnie Cochran") – first African-American member of the [Blue Angels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Angels "Blue Angels") flight demonstration squadron. Cochran made two deployments on the [USS _Enterprise_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Enterprise_(CVN-65) "USS Enterprise (CVN-65)") as an F-14 pilot with [VF-213](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-213 "VFA-213"). He later assumed command of [VF-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fighter_Squadron_1_(United_States_Navy) "Fighter Squadron 1 (United States Navy)") from 1991 until its disestablishment in 1993 and [VF-111](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-111_(1956-1995) "VF-111 (1956-1995)") from 1993 to 1994.[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-149)[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-150)
- [Joe F. Edwards Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_F._Edwards_Jr. "Joe F. Edwards Jr.") – former NASA astronaut. Edwards was awarded the [Distinguished Flying Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distinguished_Flying_Cross_(United_States) "Distinguished Flying Cross (United States)") for landing a heavily damaged F-14 on the [USS _Dwight D. Eisenhower_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Dwight_D._Eisenhower "USS Dwight D. Eisenhower") in 1991.[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-151)
- [Robert L. "Hoot" Gibson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_L._Gibson "Robert L. Gibson") – former NASA Astronaut and commander of Space Shuttle missions [STS-61-C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-61-C "STS-61-C") (1986), [STS-27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-27 "STS-27") (1988), [STS-47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-47 "STS-47") (1992) and [STS-71](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-71 "STS-71") (1995). Gibson was assigned to [VF-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fighter_Squadron_1_(United_States_Navy) "Fighter Squadron 1 (United States Navy)") during the introduction of the F-14 to fleet service between 1972 and 1975. He later became an F-14A instructor assigned to [VF-124](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-124 "VF-124") in 1977.[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-152)
- [Kara Hultgreen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara_Hultgreen "Kara Hultgreen") – one of the first female carrier-based Naval aviators. Hultgreen deployed with VF-213 on the [USS _Abraham Lincoln_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Abraham_Lincoln_(CVN-72) "USS Abraham Lincoln (CVN-72)") and became the first female fighter pilot in the U.S. military to die in a crash when her F-14 crashed into the sea on approach to the _Lincoln_ in 1994.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lohrenz-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-Lohrenz)
- [James W. Huston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_W._Huston_(author) "James W. Huston (author)") – _[New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times "New York Times")_ best-selling author. Huston flew as a RIO in many of the F-14 flight sequences in the 1980 movie _[The Final Countdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Final_Countdown_(film) "The Final Countdown (film)")_ while touring with [VF-84](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-84_(1955-1995) "VF-84 (1955-1995)") on the [USS _Nimitz_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Nimitz "USS Nimitz").[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-154)
- [Scott Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Kelly_(astronaut) "Scott Kelly (astronaut)") – former astronaut who commanded Expeditions [26](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedition_26 "Expedition 26"), [45](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedition_45 "Expedition 45"), and [46](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedition_46 "Expedition 46") of the [International Space Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Space_Station "International Space Station") (ISS) and Space Shuttle mission [STS-118](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-118 "STS-118") (2007). Kelly trained on the F-14 with [VF-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-101 "VF-101") and deployed with [VF-143](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-143 "VFA-143") on the USS _Dwight D. Eisenhower_ upon completion of his training in September 1990.[&#8202;](#ref-back-155-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-155) He also proposed a digital flight control system designed to prevent accidents similar to that of Kara Hultgreen's.[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-156)
- [Carey Lohrenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carey_Lohrenz "Carey Lohrenz") – one of the first fully qualified female naval aviators to fly the F-14. Lohrenz deployed with VF-213 on the USS _Abraham Lincoln_ in the mid-1990s before resigning from the Navy in 1999 and beginning a career as a leadership expert and professional speaker.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lohrenz-1)[\[151\]](#refnote-Lohrenz)
- [Michael Manazir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Manazir "Michael Manazir") – retired [rear admiral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rear_admiral "Rear admiral") and former vice president of [Boeing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing "Boeing") in Government Operations, Global Services, and Defense, Space and Security from 2017 to 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Manazir-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-Manazir) He first deployed in 1984 as a pilot and [landing signal officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landing_signal_officer "Landing signal officer") with [VF-51](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-51 "VF-51") before assuming command of VF-31 from 1997 to 1998 and serving as the OPNAV Staff F-14 Requirements Officer from October 1998 to November 1999. He retired from the Navy after his tenure as Deputy Chief of Naval Operations for Warfare Systems (OPNAV N9) from June 2016 to July 2017.[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-158)
- [Winston E. Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_E._Scott "Winston E. Scott") – mission specialist of Space Shuttle missions [STS-72](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-72 "STS-72") (1996) and [STS-87](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STS-87 "STS-87") (1997). Prior to his NASA career, Scott was a naval aviator who served a tour of duty with VF-84 after completing his flight training in the 1980s. He was later assigned as the Deputy Director of the Tactical Aircraft Systems Department at the Naval Air Development Center at Warminster, Pennsylvania, where he served as a research, test and evaluation (RDTE) project pilot flying the F-14, F/A-18, and A-7 aircraft.[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-159)
- [Dale "Snort" Snodgrass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Snodgrass "Dale Snodgrass") – "highest time Tomcat pilot" with more than 4,800 hours and 1,200 carrier landings with the type.[&#8202;](#ref-back-snortsnodgrass-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-snortsnodgrass)
- [Robert "Rat" Willard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Willard "Robert F. Willard") – former commander of the [U.S. Pacific Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Pacific_Fleet "United States Pacific Fleet"). As a Navy pilot, Willard frequently flew F-14s while deployed to the Western Pacific Ocean and North Arabian Sea before heading to the [Navy Fighter Weapons School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy_Strike_Fighter_Tactics_Instructor_program "United States Navy Strike Fighter Tactics Instructor program") (TOPGUN) and serving as the operations officer and executive officer. He also worked on the movie _[Top Gun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Gun "Top Gun")_ as an aerial coordinator and later assumed command of VF-51, the squadron that provided F-14s for the movie,[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-161) in the late 1980s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-162)
- [James A. Winnefeld Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_A._Winnefeld_Jr. "James A. Winnefeld Jr.") "Jaws" – former chair of the [President's Intelligence Advisory Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_Intelligence_Advisory_Board "President's Intelligence Advisory Board"). During his Navy career, Winnefeld deployed on the USS _Constellation_ with VF-24 and the [USS _Ranger_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Ranger_(CV-61) "USS Ranger (CV-61)") with VF-1 in the 1980s before becoming the commanding officer (CO) of [VF-211](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-211_(U.S._Navy) "VFA-211 (U.S. Navy)"). Later in his career, he became the CO of the USS _Enterprise_. During his time as a lieutenant, he worked on the movie _[Top Gun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Gun "Top Gun")_ as a pilot.[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-163)[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-164)[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-165)
- [Reid Wiseman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reid_Wiseman "Reid Wiseman") – NASA astronaut and flight engineer on the ISS as part of [Expedition 40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedition_40 "Expedition 40")/ [41](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedition_41 "Expedition 41") (2014) and commander of the [Artemis II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_II "Artemis II") lunar flyby mission (2026). Wiseman reported to VF-101 for training in 1999 and was subsequently assigned to VF-31 where he flew missions in support of Operations Southern Watch, Enduring Freedom, and Iraqi Freedom during two deployments in the early 2000s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-166)

## Variants

A total of 712 F-14s were built from 1969 to 1991. F-14 assembly and test flights were performed at Grumman's plant in Calverton on [Long Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Island "Long Island"), New York. Grumman facility at nearby [Bethpage, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethpage,_New_York "Bethpage, New York") was directly involved in F-14 manufacturing and was home to its engineers. The airframes were partially assembled in Bethpage and then shipped to Calverton for final assembly. Various tests were also performed at the Bethpage Plant.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### F-14A

The F-14A was the initial two-seat, twin-engine, all-weather interceptor fighter variant for the U.S. Navy. It first flew on 21 December 1970. The first 12 F-14As were prototype versions[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spickp75-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-Spickp75) (sometimes called YF-14As). Modifications late in its service life added precision strike munitions to its armament. The U.S. Navy received 558 F-14A aircraft and 79 were received by Iran.[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-168)[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-169)

Throughout its production run, the F-14A underwent numerous changes which were divided into assembly blocks labelled in multiples of 5:[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-170)

- F-14A-60 (BuNo 158612 - 158619), F-14A-65 (BuNo 158620 - 158637), F-14A-70 (BuNo 158978 - 159006), F-14A-75 (BuNo 159007 - 159025)
  - early beaver tail with dielectric fairings, IRST chin pod with ALQ-100 antenna, 7-holed gun vent
- F-14A-75/80 (BuNo 159421 - 159429), F-14A-80 (BuNo 159430 - 159468)
  - dielectric fairings removed from beaver tail
- F-14A-85 (BuNo 159588 - 159637)
  - AN/ARC-159 UHF radio replaced AN/ARC-51A radio, 2-holed gun vent first installed during construction of airframe BuNos. 159612 - 159615
- F-14A-90/95 (BuNo 159825 - 159874, 160299 - 160414)
  - small angle of attack probe added to nose radome, automated maneuvering flaps. BuNos. 160299 to 160377 were delivered to the [Imperial Iranian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_force_history_of_Iran#Imperial_era "Air force history of Iran") (IIAF).[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-171)
- F-14A-100 (BuNo 160652 - 160696), F-14A-105 (BuNo 160887 - 160937)
  - slip clutch and coupler installation added to the flap/slat system, fuel system improvements, improved AN/AWG-9, anti-corrosion measures (i.e. seals, baffles, drain plugs)
- F-14A-110 (BuNo 161138 - 161168), F-14A-115 (BuNo 161270 - 161299)
  - AN/ALQ antenna added to the beaver tail and above and below wing gloves
- F-14A-120 (BuNo 161416 - 161445), F-14A-125 (BuNo 161597 - 161626), F-14A-130 (BuNo 161850 - 161873), F-14A-135 (BuNo 162588 - 162611)
  - ECM blisters under glove vanes and on the tip of the beavertail
- F-14A-140 (BuNo 162688 - 162711)
  - TCS chin pod with ALQ-100 antenna

### F-14B

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/ab/F110_Ugello.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F110_Ugello.jpg) Close-up view of the distinctive afterburner petals of the GE F110 engine

The F-14 received its first of many major upgrades in March 1987 with the F-14A Plus (or F-14A+). The F-14A's TF30 engine was replaced with the improved F110-GE-400 engine. The F-14A+ also received the state-of-the-art [ALR-67](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ALR-67 "ALR-67") Radar Homing and Warning (RHAW) system. Many of the avionics components, as well as the AWG-9 radar, were retained. The F-14A+ was later redesignated F-14B on 1 May 1991. A total of 38 new aircraft were manufactured and 43 F-14A were upgraded into B variants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapj19p1323-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-wapj19p1323)[&#8202;](#ref-back-iapr3p52-1)[\[30\]](#refnote-iapr3p52) In the late 1990s, 81 F-14Bs were upgraded to extend airframe life and improve offensive and defensive avionics systems. The modified aircraft became known as _F-14B (Upgrade)_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-iapr3p523-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-iapr3p523)

### F-14D

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/F-14_Tomcat_ROVER_antenna_circled.jpg/500px-F-14_Tomcat_ROVER_antenna_circled.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14_Tomcat_ROVER_antenna_circled.jpg) An upgraded F-14D(R) Tomcat with the [ROVER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ROVER "ROVER") transmit antenna circled with [USS _Theodore Roosevelt_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Theodore_Roosevelt_(CVN-71) "USS Theodore Roosevelt (CVN-71)") in the background

The final variant of the F-14 was the F-14D Super Tomcat, first delivered in 1991. As with the F-14B, the F-14D was equipped with the F110-GE-400 engines. It also included newer digital avionics systems including a glass cockpit and replaced the AWG-9 with the newer [AN/APG-71](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AN/AWG-9#AN/APG-71 "AN/AWG-9") radar. Other systems included the Airborne Self Protection Jammer (ASPJ), Joint Tactical Information Distribution System (JTIDS), SJU-17(V) Naval Aircrew Common Ejection Seats (NACES), and Infrared search and track (IRST).[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-174) A total of 37 new aircraft were completed, and 18 F-14A models were upgraded to D-models, designated F-14D(R) for a rebuild. Starting in 2005, some F-14Ds received the ROVER III upgrade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-175)

### Projected variants

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Grumman_F-14B_157986_%28mfr_via_RJF%29_%2816947709020%29.jpg/500px-Grumman_F-14B_157986_%28mfr_via_RJF%29_%2816947709020%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grumman_F-14B_157986_(mfr_via_RJF)_(16947709020).jpg) A prototype F-14B test aircraft with F401 engines installed

When the F-14 was still in development, Grumman had planned an upgrade path for the Tomcat's propulsion and avionics. The first _F-14B_ was to be an improved version of the F-14A with more powerful [Pratt & Whitney F401-400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_&_Whitney_F401 "Pratt & Whitney F401") turbofans; the F-14B prototype equipped with the F401 first flew in 1973. The _F-14C_ was a projected variant of this initial F-14B with advanced multi-mission avionics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-176) Grumman also offered an interceptor version of the F-14B in response to the U.S. Air Force's Improved Manned Interceptor Program as one of the contenders to replace the [Convair F-106 Delta Dart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convair_F-106_Delta_Dart "Convair F-106 Delta Dart") as an [Aerospace Defense Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerospace_Defense_Command "Aerospace Defense Command") interceptor in the 1970s. The F-14 ADC interceptor variant was to be armed with a [GAU-7/A 25mm caseless cannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GAU-7_cannon "GAU-7 cannon") and powered by F100 turbofans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-177) The F-14B program was terminated in April 1974. The actual F-14B and D upgrades that went into service did somewhat follow the initially projected _B_ and _C_ upgrade path in practice, although it was much more delayed and with fewer airframes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-178)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/USAF_ADCOM_Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_proposed_interceptor_-_1972.jpg/500px-USAF_ADCOM_Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_proposed_interceptor_-_1972.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:USAF_ADCOM_Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_proposed_interceptor_-_1972.jpg) Grumman's proposed F-14 ADC Interceptor for USAF Aerospace Defense Command in 1972 with the simulated "Buzz Code" and Aerospace Defense Command livery and emblem on the tail

In the early 1990s, Grumman proposed a few improved _Super Tomcat_ versions. The first was the _Quickstrike_ (QS), which would have been an F-14D with navigational and targeting pods, additional attach points for weapons, and added ground attack capabilities to its radar, turning the Tomcat into a multirole [strike fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_fighter "Strike fighter"). The Quickstrike was to fill the role of the A-6 Intruder after it was retired. This was not considered enough of an improvement by Congress, so the company shifted to the _Super Tomcat 21_ (STC-21) proposed design. It was a proposed lower-cost alternative to the Navy Advanced Tactical Fighter (NATF), and later as a potential interim aircraft for the Advanced Attack (A-X) after the cancellation of the [A-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_A-12_Avenger_II "McDonnell Douglas A-12 Avenger II") attack aircraft; the STC-21 would mostly have the same shape and body as the Tomcat, and an upgraded AN/APG-71 radar. The improved [General Electric F110](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric_F110 "General Electric F110")-GE-429 engines[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[N 3\]](#refnote-179) were to provide a supercruise speed of Mach 1.3 and featured [thrust vectoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vectoring "Thrust vectoring") nozzles. The version would have reshaped leading-edge gloves, increased fuel capacity and modified control surfaces for improved takeoffs and lower landing approach speed. The _Attack Super Tomcat 21_ (ATC-21) version was the last proposed Super Tomcat design and was meant to be a more attack-oriented version of the STC-21 with possibly an [active electronically scanned array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_electronically_scanned_array "Active electronically scanned array") (AESA) radar from the canceled A-12.[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-180) The STC/ATC-21[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[N 4\]](#refnote-181) was to be able to be rebuilt from existing F-14 airframes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Donald_p9-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-Donald_p9)[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-183)[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-184)

The last "Tomcat" variant was the _ASF-14_ (Advanced Strike Fighter-14), Grumman's replacement for the NATF concept. By all accounts, it would not be even remotely related to the previous Tomcats save in appearance, incorporating the new technology and design know-how from the Advanced Tactical Fighter (ATF) and Advanced Tactical Aircraft (ATA) programs. The ASF-14 would have been a new-build aircraft with considerably greater development costs; however, its projected capabilities were not that much better than that of the STC/ATC-21 variants. Even Grumman was not enthusiastic about it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Donald_p9-1)[\[178\]](#refnote-Donald_p9) In the end, the proposed Super Tomcat variants were considered too costly and also faced stiff political opposition from the Secretary of Defense Dick Cheney. The Navy decided to pursue the cheaper F/A-18E/F Super Hornet to fill the fighter-attack or strike fighter role.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Donald_p9-2)[\[178\]](#refnote-Donald_p9)

## Operators

- [NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA "NASA") operated two F-14As. First F-14A #157991 was used High-Angle-of-Attack and Spin-Control-and-Recovery test program, between 1979 and 1985.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-194) Second F-14A #159834 at [Dryden Flight Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dryden_Flight_Research_Center "Dryden Flight Research Center") in 1986 and 1987 in a program known as the Variable-Sweep Transition Flight Experiment (VSTFE). This program explored laminar flow on variable sweep aircraft at high subsonic speeds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-195)

## Aircraft on display

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/F-14A_at_Grumman_Memorial_Park.jpg/500px-F-14A_at_Grumman_Memorial_Park.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14A_at_Grumman_Memorial_Park.jpg) An F-14A on display at [Grumman Memorial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman_Memorial_Park "Grumman Memorial Park") in New York[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/F-14_Tomcat.jpg/500px-F-14_Tomcat.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14_Tomcat.jpg) F-14A BuNo 160661 on display at the U.S. Space and Rocket Center's Aviation Challenge facility in Huntsville, Alabama, 2009[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_23.jpg/500px-Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_23.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_23.jpg) YF-14A at the [Cradle of Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cradle_of_Aviation_Museum "Cradle of Aviation Museum")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_2.JPG/500px-Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_2.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Grumman_F-14_Tomcat_2.JPG) F-14D at the [Intrepid Sea-Air-Space Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intrepid_Sea-Air-Space_Museum "Intrepid Sea-Air-Space Museum")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/F14_Tomcat_Seattle.jpg/500px-F14_Tomcat_Seattle.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F14_Tomcat_Seattle.jpg) F-14A of VF-84 "Jolly Rogers" at the [Museum of Flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Flight "Museum of Flight")

Notable F-14s preserved at museums and military installations include:

Bureau Number (BuNo) – Model – Location – SignificanceF-14A

- 157982 – [Cradle of Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cradle_of_Aviation_Museum "Cradle of Aviation Museum"), [Garden City, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_City,_New_York "Garden City, New York"); Prototype No. 3 Nonstructural demonstration testbed[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-196)
- 157984 – [National Naval Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Naval_Aviation_Museum "National Naval Aviation Museum"), [Naval Air Station Pensacola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Pensacola "Naval Air Station Pensacola"), Florida. Fifth F-14 manufactured and one of the prototypes used in flight testing. Mounted on pedestal at entrance to museum.[&#8202;](#ref-back-F-14_Tomcat/157984.-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-F-14_Tomcat/157984.)
- 157988 – [NAS Oceana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAS_Oceana "NAS Oceana") Air Park, [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia "Virginia")[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-198)
- 157990 – [March Field Air Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_Field_Air_Museum "March Field Air Museum"), [Riverside, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riverside,_California "Riverside, California"). - 159455 – [NAS Patuxent River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAS_Patuxent_River "NAS Patuxent River"), [Lexington Park, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexington_Park,_Maryland "Lexington Park, Maryland"); a former VX-23 flight test squadron aircraft\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

- 160382 – [Museum of Flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Flight "Museum of Flight"), [Tukwila, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tukwila,_Washington "Tukwila, Washington"). VF-84 "Jolly Rogers" AJ202. Stationed on the aircraft carrier USS _Nimitz_. This aircraft, as well as several other F-14As from the famous "Jolly Rogers" squadron, appear in the 1980 film _[The Final Countdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Final_Countdown_(film) "The Final Countdown (film)")_, which was filmed on board USS _Nimitz_. On loan from the National Museum of Naval Aviation at Pensacola, Florida.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-216)

F-14D(R)

- 159600 – Fort Worth Aviation Museum, [Fort Worth, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Worth,_Texas "Fort Worth, Texas"). On loan from the National Naval Aviation Museum, Pensacola, Florida. Nicknamed "Christine", it was the longest-serving F-14 Tomcat in U.S. Navy. Remanufactured from F-14A to F-14D(R) configuration, it was originally built in 1976 and made the final combat deployment/cruise of the F-14 in 2006.[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-251)
- 159610 – Smithsonian [National Air and Space Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Air_and_Space_Museum "National Air and Space Museum"), [Steven F. Udvar-Hazy Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_F._Udvar-Hazy_Center "Steven F. Udvar-Hazy Center"), [Chantilly, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chantilly,_Virginia "Chantilly, Virginia"). This F-14 was one of those involved in the [second Gulf of Sidra incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Sidra_incident_(1989) "Gulf of Sidra incident (1989)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-252)
- 159619 – [Florida Air Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Air_Museum "Florida Air Museum") at Sun 'n Fun, [Lakeland Linder International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakeland_Linder_International_Airport "Lakeland Linder International Airport"), [Lakeland, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakeland,_Florida "Lakeland, Florida")\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]
- 159629 - Veterans Park, [Kenner, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenner,_Louisiana "Kenner, Louisiana")
- 161159 – [National Naval Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Naval_Aviation_Museum "National Naval Aviation Museum"), [Naval Air Station Pensacola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Pensacola "Naval Air Station Pensacola"), Florida. Completed the last combat flight and the last combat carrier arrested landing (trap) by a U.S. Navy F-14.[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-253)
- 161163 – [Prairie Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie_Aviation_Museum "Prairie Aviation Museum"), [Bloomington, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomington,_Illinois "Bloomington, Illinois"). Depot Level Conversion performed September 1991. Retired as MODEX 205 of Fighter Squadron 213 (VF-213), Black Lions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-254)
- 161166 – [Carolinas Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolinas_Aviation_Museum "Carolinas Aviation Museum"), [Charlotte, North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte,_North_Carolina "Charlotte, North Carolina")[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-255)

On loan from National Museum of Naval Aviation, Pensacola, Florida. Last Tomcat to operationally trap aboard a U.S. Navy aircraft carrier.[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-265) It was previously displayed at [Virginian Aviation Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Aviation_Museum "Virginia Aviation Museum") before being relocated to the current site upon the museum's closure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-266)
- 164350 – [Joe Davies Heritage Airpark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Davies_Heritage_Airpark "Joe Davies Heritage Airpark") at Palmdale Plant 42, [Palmdale, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmdale,_California "Palmdale, California")\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

- 164603 – Cradle of Aviation Museum, Garden City, New York. Felix 101 from VF-31 is the last Tomcat to fly in U.S. Navy service. Last F-14 manufactured, assigned to VX-4, later VX-9, at Naval Air Station Point Mugu, California during its operational service and used the callsign "Vandy 1".\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

## Specifications (F-14D)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/F-14D.jpg/500px-F-14D.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F-14D.jpg) Schematic diagram (F-14D)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/F14_2_Wiki.jpg/500px-F14_2_Wiki.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F14_2_Wiki.jpg) F-14A of VF 111 "Sundowners" (USS _Carl Vinson_)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/AIM-54_6_Pack.jpg/500px-AIM-54_6_Pack.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AIM-54_6_Pack.jpg) F-14B from the VF-211 Fighting Checkmates carrying six [AIM-54 Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-54_Phoenix "AIM-54 Phoenix") missiles[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/At_the_Intrepid_Museum_2023_123.jpg/500px-At_the_Intrepid_Museum_2023_123.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:At_the_Intrepid_Museum_2023_123.jpg) Intrepid Museum display showing Sidewinder (upper), Sparrow (middle) and Phoenix (bottom) missiles

_Data from_ U.S. Navy file,[&#8202;](#ref-back-USN_FF-2)[\[2\]](#refnote-USN_FF)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SAC_F-14D-1)[\[61\]](#refnote-SAC_F-14D) Spick,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spick_p81-2)[\[56\]](#refnote-Spick_p81) Flight International March 1985[&#8202;](#ref-back-Flight300385p19-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-Flight300385p19)

**General characteristics**

- **Crew:** 2 (pilot and radar intercept officer)
- **Length:** 62ft 9in (19.13m)
- **Wingspan:** 64ft 1.5in (19.545m)
- **Swept wingspan:** 38ft 2.5in (11.646m) swept
- **Height:** 16ft (4.9m)
- **Wing area:** 565sqft (52.5m2) wings only

  - 1,008sqft (94m2) effective area including fuselage[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-7)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution)
- **[Airfoil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airfoil "Airfoil"):**

  - Grumman (1.74)(35)(9.6)-(1.1)(30)(1.1) root
  - Grumman (1.27)(30)(9.0)-(1.1)(40)(1.1) tip[&#8202;](#ref-back-Selig-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-Selig)
- **Empty weight:** 43,735lb (19,838kg)
- **Gross weight:** 61,000lb (27,669kg)
- **Max takeoff weight:** 74,350lb (33,725kg)
- **Fuel capacity:** 16,200lb (7,348kg) internal fuel; 2 × optional 267USgal (222impgal; 1,010L) / 1,756lb (797kg) external drop tanks[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spick_p81-3)[\[56\]](#refnote-Spick_p81)
- **Powerplant:** 2 × [General Electric F110](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric_F110 "General Electric F110")-GE-400 [afterburning turbofans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afterburning_turbofan "Afterburning turbofan"), 16,333lbf (72.65kN) thrust eachdry, 26,950lbf (119.9kN) with afterburner[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[N 5\]](#refnote-273)

**Performance**

- **Maximum speed:** Mach 2.34, 1,544mph (1,342kn; 2,485km/h) at 40,000 ft (12,190 m)

  - Mach 1.2, 794kn (914mph; 1,470km/h) at sea level
- **Cruise speed:** 420kn (480mph, 780km/h)
- **Stall speed:** 118kn (136mph, 219km/h) power-off
- **Range:** 1,600nmi (1,800mi, 3,000km)
- **Combat range:** 503nmi (579mi, 932km) fighter escort

  - 204nmi (235mi; 378km) deck launched intercept
  - 150nmi (170mi; 280km) fleet air defense with 1.91 hours of loiter[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[N 6\]](#refnote-274)
- **Service ceiling:** 53,000ft (16,000m) plus
- **g limits:** +7.5 g (+6.5 g operational limit)[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[N 7\]](#refnote-275)
- **Rate of climb:** 45,000ft/min (230m/s) plus
- **Wing loading:** 96lb/sqft (470kg/m2)

  - 48lb/sqft (230kg/m2) effective[&#8202;](#ref-back-mc_f14designevolution-8)[\[48\]](#refnote-mc_f14designevolution)
- **[Thrust/weight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust-to-weight_ratio "Thrust-to-weight ratio"):** 0.88 at gross weight (1.02 with loaded weight & 50% internal fuel)

- **Takeoff roll:** 2,500ft (760m)
- **Landing roll:** 2,400ft (730m)

**Armament**

- **Guns:** 1× [20 mm (0.787 in)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_mm_caliber "20 mm caliber") [M61A1 Vulcan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M61_Vulcan "M61 Vulcan") 6-barreled rotary cannon, with 675 rounds
- **Hardpoints:** 10 total: 6× under-fuselage, 2× under [nacelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacelle "Nacelle") and 2× on wing gloves[&#8202;](#ref-back-Spick_p112-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-Spick_p112)[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[N 8\]](#refnote-277) with a capacity of 14,500lb (6,600kg) of ordnance and fuel tanks[&#8202;](#ref-back-baug_F-14D-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-baug_F-14D), with provisions to carry combinations of:

  - **Rockets:** 7x [LAU-10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LAU-10 "LAU-10") rocket pods (for a total of 28 rockets)
  - **Missiles:** [AIM-54 Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-54_Phoenix "AIM-54 Phoenix"), [AIM-7 Sparrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-7_Sparrow "AIM-7 Sparrow"), [AIM-9 Sidewinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-9_Sidewinder "AIM-9 Sidewinder") air-to-air missiles[&#8202;](#ref-back-279-0)[\[N 9\]](#refnote-279)
  - **Bombs:**

    - [JDAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Direct_Attack_Munition "Joint Direct Attack Munition") [precision-guided munition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precision-guided_munition "Precision-guided munition") (PGMs)
    - [Paveway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paveway "Paveway") series of [laser-guided bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laser-guided_bomb "Laser-guided bomb")
    - [Mk 80 series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_80_bomb_series "Mark 80 bomb series") of unguided [iron bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity_bomb "Gravity bomb")
    - [Mk 20 Rockeye II cluster munition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBU-100_Cluster_Bomb "CBU-100 Cluster Bomb")
  - **Other:**

    - [Tactical Airborne Reconnaissance Pod System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tactical_Airborne_Reconnaissance_Pod_System "Tactical Airborne Reconnaissance Pod System") (TARPS)
    - [LANTIRN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LANTIRN "LANTIRN") Targeting System (LTS) pod (AN/AAQ-14)
    - 2× 267USgal (1,010L; 222impgal) [drop tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drop_tank "Drop tank") for extended range/loitering time

**Avionics**

- [Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hughes_Aircraft "Hughes Aircraft") [AN/APG-71](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AN/AWG-9 "AN/AWG-9") radar
- [AN/ALR-67](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AN/ALR-67 "AN/ALR-67") radar warning receiver
- AN/AAS-42 [infrared search and track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_search_and_track "Infrared search and track"), AAX-1 [TCS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_Camera_System?action=edit&redlink=1 "Television Camera System")
- AN/ASN-130 [Inertial navigation system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inertial_navigation_system "Inertial navigation system")
- [Remotely Operated Video Enhanced Receiver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ROVER "ROVER") (ROVER) upgrade

## Tomcat logo

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/3/31/Original_F14_Tomcat_logo.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Original_F14_Tomcat_logo.jpg) Tomcat logo

The Tomcat logo design came when Grumman's Director of Presentation Services, Dick Milligan, and one of his artists, Grumman employee Jim Rodriguez, were asked for a logo by Grumman's Director of Business Development and former Blue Angels No. 5 pilot, Norm Gandia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-f-14assoc-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-f-14assoc) Per Rodriguez, "He asked me to draw a lifelike Tomcat wearing boxing gloves and trunks sporting a six-shooter on his left side, where the guns are located on the F-14, along with two tails." The cat was drawn up after a tabby cat was sourced, used for photographs, and named "Tom". The logo has gone through many variations, including one for the then– [Imperial Iranian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Iranian_Air_Force "Imperial Iranian Air Force") F-14, called "Ali-cat". The accompanying slogan, "Anytime Baby!" was developed by Norm Gandia as a challenge to the U.S. Air Force's McDonnell Douglas F-15 Eagle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-f-14assoc-1)[\[271\]](#refnote-f-14assoc)[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-281)

## Notable appearances in media

Main article: [Aircraft in fiction §F-14 Tomcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_in_fiction#F-14_Tomcat "Aircraft in fiction")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/VF-111_TOPGUN_MOVIE.jpg/500px-VF-111_TOPGUN_MOVIE.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:VF-111_TOPGUN_MOVIE.jpg) F-14A Tomcats of Fighter Squadrons [VF-51](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-51 "VF-51") "Screaming Eagles" and [VF-111](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-111_(1956-95) "VF-111 (1956-95)") "Sundowners" in formation with [F-5E/F Tiger IIs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_F-5 "Northrop F-5"). The squadrons provided F-14s for filming aerial sequences in the movie _[Top Gun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Gun "Top Gun")_.

The Grumman F-14 Tomcat was central to the 1986 film _[Top Gun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Gun "Top Gun")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Star_Quality-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-Star_Quality)[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-283)[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-284) The aviation-themed film was such a success in creating interest in naval aviation that the US Navy, which assisted with the film, set up recruitment desks outside some theaters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-285-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-285) Producers paid the US Navy $886,000(equivalent to $2,652,000 in 2025) as reimbursement for flight time of aircraft in the film with an F-14 billed at $7,600(equivalent to $22,800 in 2025) per flight hour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-286)[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-287) The F-14 Tomcat was also featured in the sequel, _[Top Gun: Maverick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Gun:_Maverick "Top Gun: Maverick")_ (2022).[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-288) Two F-14As of [VF-84](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-84_(1955-95) "VF-84 (1955-95)") from [USS _Nimitz_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Nimitz "USS Nimitz") were featured in the 1980 film _[The Final Countdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Final_Countdown_(film) "The Final Countdown (film)")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-289-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-289) with four from the squadron in the 1996 release _[Executive Decision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Decision "Executive Decision")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-290) The television series [JAG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JAG_(TV_series) "JAG (TV series)") frequently used stock footage of the F-14 from other media, including Top Gun.[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-291) Multiple F-14s are featured in the 2008 documentary _[Speed & Angels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speed_&_Angels "Speed & Angels")_, featuring the story of two young Navy officers working to achieve their dream of becoming F-14 fighter pilots.[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-292) The F-14 served as an inspiration for various fictional aircraft, most notably the _[Macross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macross "Macross")_ franchise's [VF-1 Valkyrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VF-1_Valkyrie "VF-1 Valkyrie") and the Skystriker XP-14F from the _[G.I. Joe: A Real American Hero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G.I._Joe:_A_Real_American_Hero "G.I. Joe: A Real American Hero")_ toyline.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Luke_Plunkett-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-Luke_Plunkett) Actual F-14s were featured in the first episode of _[Macross Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macross_Zero "Macross Zero")_, the [OVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OVA "OVA") prequel to _[Super Dimension Fortress Macross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Dimension_Fortress_Macross "Super Dimension Fortress Macross")_ (adapted as _[Robotech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robotech "Robotech")_ in the US).[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-294)

## References

### Notes

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-14)[↑](#ref-back-14-0)Admiral Thomas F. Connolly wrote the chapter, "The TFX – One Fighter For All".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Woolridge-2)[\[12\]](#refnote-Woolridge)
2. [&#8202;](#refnote-65)[↑](#ref-back-65-0)The F-14's maximum speed is limited by the scheduling of the inlet ramps, and the inlet ramp programming for the F110 was optimized more for transonic performance; at higher speeds, the installed dynamic thrust of the TF30 actually exceeds the F110's.
3. [&#8202;](#refnote-179)[↑](#ref-back-179-0)The F110-GE-429 is the [designation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_military_aircraft_engine_designations "United States military aircraft engine designations") of the proposed Navy version of the F110-GE-129.
4. [&#8202;](#refnote-181)[↑](#ref-back-181-0)The STC-21 and ATC-21 are also sometimes informally referred to as ST-21 and AST-21 respectively.
5. [&#8202;](#refnote-273)[↑](#ref-back-273-0)These figures are static, sea-level uninstalled thrust. Static, sea-level installed thrust is 13,800lbf (61kN) at military power and 23,600lbf (105kN) in full afterburner. At Mach 0.9 at sea-level, installed thrust is 30,200lbf (134kN) in full afterburner.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NAVAIR-1)[\[62\]](#refnote-NAVAIR)
6. [&#8202;](#refnote-274)[↑](#ref-back-274-0)All figures are for external stores loading of 2× AIM-54, 2× AIM-7, 2× AIM-9, 2× 267 U.S. gal fuel tanks.
7. [&#8202;](#refnote-275)[↑](#ref-back-275-0)The g limit was operationally limited by the U.S. Navy to +6.5 g to increase service life.
8. [&#8202;](#refnote-277)[↑](#ref-back-277-0) The hardpoints between nacelles include two on centerline plus four others next to nacelles. Points between nacelles can only carry a maximum of four missiles at one time. Each wing glove can carry one large pylon for larger missiles, with one rail on the outboard side of the pylon for a Sidewinder.
9. [&#8202;](#refnote-279)[↑](#ref-back-279-0)Loading configurations:

   - 2× AIM-9 + 6× AIM-54 (Rarely used due to weight stress on airframe)
   - 2× AIM-9 + 2× AIM-54 + 3× AIM-7 (Most common load during Cold War era)
   - 2× AIM-9 + 4× AIM-54 + 2× AIM-7
   - 2× AIM-9 + 6× AIM-7
   - 4× AIM-9 + 4× AIM-54
   - 4× AIM-9 + 4× AIM-7

[_Immortal, Updated Edition: A Military History of Iran and Its Armed Forces_](https://books.google.com/books?id=MOuVAgAAQBAJ&pg=PA272). Georgetown University Press. p.272\. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [9781626160323](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/9781626160323 "Special:BookSources/9781626160323"). 082. [↑](#ref-back-84-0)Taghvaee, Babak (23 August 2018). ["New Claws for the Persian Cats"](https://www.key.aero/article/new-claws-persian-cats). _Key.Aero_. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201231034005/https://www.key.aero/article/new-claws-persian-cats) from the original on 31 December 2020. Retrieved 30 December 2020. Integration of AIM-9J and AIM-7E-2 with the Tomcat's weapons system was a temporary solution for Iran and because of that, the deputy of Industrial Research and SSJ started working in the 1990s on plans for integrating the Russian Vympel R-27R medium-range semi-active radar homing AAM with the AWG-9 radar. The project was eventually abandoned because of insurmountable technical issues. There was a similar project to integrate the short-range Vympel R-73E with the F-14's ire control system but this didn't work because of the lack of an infrared search and track (IRST) system on Iran's Tomcats. The missile's performance when used in conjunction with an F-14 was much inferior to that achieved when it was launched from a MiG-29. Launching the R-73E without input from an IRST reduced the missile's range to less than what Tomcat's could achieve with their ageing AIM-9Js.
